- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2025). Effects of punishment driven by inequity aversion on promoting cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment driven by inequity aversion can promote the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment costs are low and fines are high. Inequity-averse cooperators (IACs) can invade defectors and dominate the population under favorable parameter regimes, but their effectiveness is limited by internal conflict (punishing other cooperators or IACs) and the presence of non-punishing cooperators. Inequity-averse defectors (IADs) can also promote cooperation by forming protective barriers around cooperators, reducing the ability of defectors to invade. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of fine to cost, the synergy factor (MPCR), and the spatial structure. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms—specifically, those based on inequity aversion—can increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, particularly when punishment is not too costly and fines are substantial. The findings suggest that the design of punishment (who is targeted, how costs and fines scale) and the spatial structure of interactions are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it supports the idea that punishment can change the behavioral composition of the group, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related models, but this link is not demonstrated here.

- source: Lv, S. J., & Song, F. F. (2022). Particle swarm intelligence and the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game with punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of punishment on cooperation in a spatial public goods game depends on both the punishment intensity and the learning rule (PSO weighting coefficient). For low punishment intensity, low susceptibility to neighbors (low omega) promotes cooperation; for high punishment intensity, high omega is more beneficial. Intermediate omega values increase punishment input but can reduce cooperation. The enhancement factor (r) also moderates these effects. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are about cooperation rates and investment allocation, not direct welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and learning rules affect cooperation rates and investment allocation in spatial PGGs with continuous strategies. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment. For the prediction task, the main value is in understanding that the effect of punishment on cooperation is highly sensitive to punishment intensity and the learning rule, and that the allocation of resources between cooperation and punishment can affect the expansion of cooperative clusters. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's findings should be used only as indirect, mechanistic context rather than as direct quantitative evidence for efficiency prediction.

- source: Kang, H. W., Liu, S. X., Chen, Q. Y., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). Bonus-based mercenary punishment promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Heliyon*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that a bonus-based mercenary punishment mechanism can substantially increase the proportion of cooperators in a public goods game, compared to the standard game without punishment. The effect is sensitive to the size of the bonus, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the allocation of fines collected from defectors. There is an optimal range for the bonus: too little fails to support punishers, too much burdens cooperators. Allocating more fines to punishers (rather than cooperators) is more effective in sustaining cooperation. The model is simulated on a network, and the main reported outcomes are the proportions of each strategy over time and under different parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with bonus support for punishers) increases cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the parameters are tuned appropriately. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (with proper support) is likely to increase cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but this link is not directly quantified here. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative and parametric effects of punishment and bonus mechanisms on cooperation prevalence.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Wang, Z. Y., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Negative Feedback Punishment Approach Helps Sanctioning Institutions Achieve Stable, Time-Saving and Low-Cost Performances. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The negative feedback punishment approach, in which punishment intensity decreases as group cooperation increases, leads to more stable and higher cooperation rates, requires less time to reach high cooperation, and results in lower cumulative punishment intensity (interpreted as lower cost) compared to constant or positive feedback punishment methods. Theoretical analysis shows that this approach allows for stable intermediate levels of cooperation, while constant or positive feedback methods tend to produce unstable or all-or-nothing outcomes. The findings are based on evolutionary simulations of a public goods game with pool punishment, but do not report efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment rule (specifically, negative feedback between cooperation and punishment intensity) can substantially affect the stability and cost of achieving high cooperation in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that negative feedback punishment rules may reduce the cost (in terms of punishment applied) and time to reach high cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment rule design (punishment_tech) and dynamic adjustment can moderate the behavioral and cost outcomes of punishment institutions.

- source: Shin, H. C., Vallury, S., Janssen, M. A., & Yu, D. J. (2022). Joint effects of voluntary participation and group selection on the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of voluntary participation on the evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment is asymmetric: in large groups and for a moderate range of nonparticipant payoffs, voluntary participation can increase the long-run frequency of cooperation compared to compulsory participation. If the nonparticipant payoff is too low, voluntary participation has little effect; if too high, it sharply reduces cooperation. In small groups, voluntary participation does not increase cooperation for any nonparticipant payoff. The model tracks frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a public goods game with punishment and voluntary participation, showing that the effect of enabling voluntary participation (an 'exit' option) depends on group size and the outside option's payoff. While the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, higher frequencies of cooperation (especially in large groups with moderate outside options) suggest that efficiency could be higher in those cases. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the results should be used as informative indirect evidence: enabling voluntary participation may increase efficiency only in large groups and only if the outside option is not too attractive or too poor. The model's structure and parameterization are relevant for mapping to game design dimensions, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Quan, J., Guo, H. T., & Wang, X. J. (2022). Impact of reputation-based switching strategy between punishment and social exclusion on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that conditional punishment based on reputation (switching between fines and exclusion) is more effective at promoting cooperation than traditional punishment alone. Higher reputation thresholds (stricter punishment), higher fines, and lower exclusion costs all lead to higher frequencies of cooperation. The enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) also strongly moderates the emergence and stability of cooperation. The model shows phase transitions between full defection and full cooperation, and regions where cooperation and defection coexist. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes (strategy frequencies).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms (including conditionality based on reputation, the severity of fines, and the cost of exclusion) can substantially affect the frequency of cooperation in spatial PGGs. While the findings are highly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on cooperation rates, they are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency or group payoff, since these are not reported. The results suggest that, in similar spatial PGGs, enabling punishment (especially with flexible, reputation-based mechanisms) is likely to increase cooperation rates, which would generally be expected to improve efficiency, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in optional public goods games with loners' punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of loners who punish cooperators (LP) into the optional public goods game leads to new evolutionary phases, including three-strategy cyclic states (C+D+LP), pure cooperator phases, and coexistence of cooperators with both types of loners. The presence and intensity of punishment (punishment cost and fine) modulate the transitions between these phases. Under certain conditions (e.g., high punishment fine and/or cost, high loner income), the system can escape the typical cyclic dominance and reach a pure cooperation phase or a coexistence phase where cooperators are protected by traditional loners from punishing loners. However, the paper does not quantify the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence and stability of cooperation as a strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how punishment by loners affects the prevalence of cooperation in optional public goods games, under varying game parameters (synergy factor, loner income, punishment cost, punishment fine). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for understanding how game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, loner income) affect the likelihood of cooperation dominating or persisting, which may be indirectly informative for efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates translate to higher efficiency. Caution is warranted, as the mapping from strategy frequencies to efficiency is not explicit in this work.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Li, B. F., Li, C., Li, H. B., & Zheng, Z. M. (2022). Two-layer network model of public goods games with intervention and corruption. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment (umpire intervention) always increases the fraction of cooperators compared to no intervention, even if all umpires are corrupt and only accept bribes. The fraction of fair umpires, the spatial distribution of umpires, the punishment fine, and the bribe size all have strong effects on cooperation rates. In particular, higher bribe costs and higher punishment fines both increase cooperation, but the effect of bribe cost is more pronounced. The model is robust to network structure (square lattice vs. scale-free). However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment mechanism (even if imperfect or corrupt) increases cooperation rates in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates as a function of punishment design, but not for predicting group welfare or surplus. The model's detailed exploration of punishment cost, effectiveness, and corruption is informative for mechanism design, but the lack of payoff reporting is a key limitation for efficiency prediction.

- source: Kang, H. W., Xu, Y. H., Chen, Q. Y., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). The role of reputation to reduce punishment costs in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based punishment mechanism in a spatial public goods game model allows punishers to reduce their punishment costs as their reputation increases. Simulation results show that this mechanism enables the system to achieve global cooperation and expel betrayers even at lower synergy factors, where traditional punishment fails. The model demonstrates that clusters of punishers with high reputation can resist and eliminate betrayers more effectively, and that parameter ranges (synergy factor, punishment cost, fine) determine the system's ability to reach cooperation. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of strategies and the system's convergence to cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that reducing punishment costs via a reputation mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the marginal per-capita return (synergy factor) is low. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms with lower or dynamically reduced costs (e.g., via reputation) are likely to yield higher efficiency than traditional, high-cost punishment, particularly in low-MPCR environments. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment cost and reputation mechanisms on cooperation dynamics.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). The evolution of cooperation and punishment in spatial public goods games with bribery. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with peer punishment, the introduction of bribery (defectors bribing punishers to avoid sanctions, and punishers accepting bribes) leads to complex phase transitions among strategies. When punishment intensity is low, bribery can increase the fraction of contributors (interpreted as higher cooperation), as bribe-recipients and bribers form alliances that resist defectors. As the bribe amount increases, the system can enter cyclic coexistence states involving bribers, bribe-recipients, and defectors. When punishment intensity is high, bribery undermines the maintenance of punishment and can reduce the contribution level. The effect of bribery is highly sensitive to the synergy coefficient (analogous to MPCR), punishment fine, and bribe amount. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of strategies and contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and bribery mechanisms interact to affect cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by proxy, efficiency) is moderated by the possibility of bribery and the parameters of the game (synergy coefficient, punishment fine, bribe amount). In environments where punishment is weak, bribery can increase cooperation; where punishment is strong, bribery can undermine it. However, since efficiency is not directly measured, these results should be used cautiously for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model is also spatial and evolutionary, which may differ from typical lab PGGs.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Supporting punishment via taxation in a structured population. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with tax-supported punishment, the prevalence of cooperation, defection, and punishment strategies depends sensitively on the synergy factor, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. When the tax exceeds the cost of punishment, punishing cooperators can dominate. At low tax and high punishment cost, defectors dominate. Intermediate regimes can produce coexistence of multiple strategies, including cyclic dominance. The spatial structure allows for richer dynamics than well-mixed models, including new three-strategy coexistence states. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies depends on game design dimensions such as player group size, number of strategies, spatial structure, tax level, punishment cost, and fine. However, because it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding which parameter regimes are likely to support cooperation or complex coexistence, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates correspond to higher efficiency, but this link is not established or quantified in the paper.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Facilitating the evolution of cooperation through altruistic punishment with adaptive feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing adaptive feedback in punishment intensity—where punishment increases as defection rises and decreases as cooperation rises—can facilitate the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. The system can exhibit bistability (converging to either full cooperation with low punishment or full defection with high punishment), stable oscillations between cooperation and defection, or stable full cooperation/defection depending on the average per capita incentive and other parameters. Notably, full cooperation can be achieved and maintained with only low-intensity punishment under certain conditions. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of the replicator dynamics with feedback, not on empirical or experimental data. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so implications for efficiency must be inferred indirectly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that adaptive, feedback-based punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public goods games, sometimes with only low punishment intensity. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that under certain parameter regimes, adaptive punishment could lead to high efficiency (if cooperation is high and punishment costs are low). However, the absence of direct efficiency analysis means that predictions about efficiency effects must be made cautiously and indirectly, based on the reported cooperation dynamics. The model is most relevant for settings with institutional punishment, adaptive feedback, and infinite well-mixed populations, and less directly applicable to finite, experimental, or empirical settings.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Wang, H. Z., & Jiang, Y. L. (2023). Multi-heterogeneity public goods evolutionary game on lattice. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a modified pool punishment mechanism and aspiration-driven updating in a multi-heterogeneity public goods game on a lattice can promote cooperation under certain conditions. Specifically, moderate punishment and low aspiration factor are most effective at increasing the fraction of cooperators. Too high punishment or aspiration factor leads to instability or loss of cooperation. The results are robust to different lattice sizes and initial conditions. While average payoffs for cooperators and defectors are reported, the main focus is on cooperation rates, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games with multiple heterogeneities, but only under moderate punishment and low aspiration factor. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic: moderate punishment may improve outcomes, but excessive punishment can reduce both cooperation and payoffs. The lattice structure and aspiration-driven updating are important contextual factors.

- source: Feng, S. A., Liu, X. S., & Dong, Y. D. (2022). Limited punishment pool may promote cooperation in the public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a limited punishment pool in a public goods game can effectively suppress defectors and promote cooperation, especially when the pool's capacity is set within an optimal range. If the punishment pool is too small, defectors persist; if it is too large, punishers crowd out cooperators. The model shows that the right balance in punishment pool capacity maximizes the prevalence of cooperation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that the 'overall income' is higher in certain parameter ranges where both cooperators and punishers coexist and defectors are suppressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on the capacity of the punishment pool: too little punishment is ineffective, but too much can reduce cooperation by crowding out cooperators. For prediction tasks, the results imply that the design of the punishment mechanism (especially its capacity and cost parameters) is crucial for maximizing cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Zhang, Y. Q., Zheng, Z. H., Zhang, X. M., & Ma, J. L. (2025). Dynamic punishment-reputation synergy drives cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based cost reduction for punishers in a spatial public goods game with dynamic probabilistic punishment greatly enhances the evolution and stability of cooperation. Higher increments to punisher reputation (T) further promote cooperation and accelerate convergence to full cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even when the cost of punishment exceeds the fine imposed on defectors, and that higher initial punishment probabilities speed up the emergence of cooperation. The effect of the maximum reputation threshold is minimal. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games with dynamic punishment and reputation-based cost reduction, punishment mechanisms can robustly increase cooperation rates, even under adverse cost conditions. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The payoff structure is specified, so in principle, efficiency could be inferred, but the paper does not report these results. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides only indirect support, suggesting that conditions which increase cooperation rate (such as reputation-based cost reduction for punishers) are likely to also increase efficiency, but this is not demonstrated directly.

- source: Ling, T., Li, Z., Feng, M. Y., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Supervised cooperation on interdependent public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that both higher punishment severity (fine ratio) and higher bribe costs increase the fraction of cooperators and fair referees in a spatial PGG with coevolving referee behavior. There are rapid phase transitions from defection/corruption to cooperation/fairness as these parameters increase. The synergy factor (MPCR analog) is a key moderator: higher values make cooperation and fairness more likely. The supervision fee for referees also affects the prevalence of fairness. Payoff distributions are shown in spatial snapshots, indicating that average player payoffs increase with the synergy factor, but the paper does not systematically quantify efficiency or compare group payoffs between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. The main findings are about behavioral outcomes (cooperation, fairness) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms (with coevolving referee behavior) can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, especially when punishment is severe and bribes are costly. However, for the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency (group payoff relative to full cooperation) as a function of game design and control efficiency—the paper is only indirectly informative. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it provide a direct baseline comparison with and without punishment. The qualitative discussion and payoff snapshots suggest that higher cooperation rates are associated with higher average payoffs, but the lack of explicit efficiency metrics limits direct applicability for quantitative prediction. The model structure and parameter dependencies (e.g., on player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are informative for mechanism understanding.

- source: Qian, J., Zhang, T. D., Zhang, Y. F., Chai, Y. T., Sun, X., & Wang, Z. (2023). The construction of peer punishment preference: how central power shapes prosocial and antisocial punishment behaviors. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that the effectiveness and efficiency of central power (e.g., the Rule of Law) determine the prevalence of antisocial punishment in a population, while prosocial punishment remains stable. Antisocial punishment is highest at intermediate levels of central power and suppressed when central power is either very strong or very weak. The population can be decomposed into three basic types (Collaboration Enthusiast, Fence Sitter, Rational Bystander), and the central power shifts the proportions of these types, which in turn determines the observed punishment and contribution behaviors. The model is validated at the 'phenomenal level' by comparison to cross-regional experimental data, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how institutional context (central power) shapes the behavioral landscape of punishment and cooperation in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for predicting how changes in central authority or social norms might shift the prevalence of antisocial punishment and, indirectly, contribution rates. For direct efficiency prediction, empirical or simulation studies that report group payoff or efficiency are needed.

- source: Quan, J., Cui, S. H., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Reputation-based probabilistic punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing reputation-based probabilistic punishment into the spatial public goods game increases the frequency of cooperation and average payoffs. Higher reputation thresholds (meaning more defectors are likely to be punished), higher punishment intensity (lower b), and higher punishment fines (beta) all lead to higher cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model shows that cooperative and punishing strategies form spatial clusters with higher reputations and higher payoffs, while defectors cluster in low-reputation, low-payoff regions. The effect on efficiency is inferred from the increase in average payoff and the reduction of defectors, but efficiency as a normalized group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that, in spatial public goods games, enabling punishment (especially when it is reputation-based and probabilistic) increases cooperation and average payoffs compared to control conditions without punishment. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with higher intensity, lower cost, or higher fines) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment mechanism targets low-reputation defectors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency as a normalized group payoff, quantitative predictions should be made with caution and may require mapping from average payoff or cooperation rate. The results are most applicable to spatial, agent-based settings with reputation dynamics and probabilistic punishment.

- source: Odouard, V. V., Smirnova, D., & Edelman, S. (2023). Polarize, catalyze, and stabilize: how a minority of norm internalizers amplify group selection and punishment. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of norm internalizers in the strategy space (even as a small minority, around 10% of the population) leads to higher mean cooperation rates, longer and more stable periods of high cooperation, and fewer long periods of low cooperation, compared to conditions with only group selection and punishment. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios, where cooperation is possible but not guaranteed. The mechanism is indirect: norm internalizers polarize groups (increasing inter-group variance), catalyze spikes in cooperation when global cooperation is low, and stabilize high-cooperation states. The effect is not due to norm internalizers themselves cooperating more, but to their influence on the behavior of other agents. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of public goods games with group selection and imperfect punishment, adding a minority of norm internalizers can substantially increase mean cooperation rates, especially at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency: higher cooperation rates likely imply higher efficiency, but the mapping is not quantified here. The model assumes punishment is imperfect (not all defections are observed), and punishment is implemented as withholding benefits rather than costly punishment. The results are robust across two different model structures (abstract and spatial/naturalistic). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of norm internalization mechanisms can amplify the effect of punishment and group selection on cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling punishment. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that sampling punishment can dramatically increase the average cooperation level in both linear (PGG) and nonlinear (CRD) social dilemmas, provided the punishment intensity is above a threshold. Low punishment thresholds and small sample sizes are optimal, as they maximize cooperation and minimize punishment cost. The effect of punishment intensity is nonlinear: below a threshold, it is ineffective, but above the threshold, further increases yield little additional benefit. These findings are robust across both PGG and CRD models. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment mechanisms—specifically, sampling punishment—can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is sufficiently intense, the threshold for triggering punishment is low, and the sample size is small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. If the downstream prediction task assumes a strong link between cooperation and efficiency, these results may be indirectly informative, but caution is warranted.

- source: Guo, P., Wang, X. N., & Zhang, D. (2023). Optimization of supervision mechanism for public cooperation considering limited supervision ability and heterogeneous preferences. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that targeted punishment via the HPS mechanism increases the fraction of cooperators compared to random supervision, especially at higher synergy factors and with well-chosen exemption list parameters. Increasing supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list capacity generally increases cooperation rates. Moderately long observation periods and short removal times also promote cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that targeted punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, and that design parameters such as supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list management are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results may be useful for predicting the direction of change in cooperation rates under different punishment regimes, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Zhuo, S. Q., Liu, J., Ren, T. Y., & Sun, J. Y. (2022). Evolution dynamics with the switching strategy of punishment and expulsion in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that a switching strategy—punishing most defectors and expelling a few—can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the cost of punishment, the threshold for switching between punishment and expulsion, and the population density. Lower punishment costs, lower switching thresholds, and intermediate population densities maximize cooperation rates. The model demonstrates that combining expulsion and punishment is more effective at promoting cooperation than either strategy alone. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and expulsion) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, and that the effect depends on design parameters such as punishment cost, threshold, and population density. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, under certain conditions, punishment and expulsion can increase cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not demonstrated or quantified in the paper.

- source: Zhang, G., Yao, Y. C., Zeng, Z. Y., Feng, M. Y., & Chica, M. (2025). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game with tolerant punishment based on reputation threshold. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment targeted at defectors in low-reputation groups, especially with a high reputation threshold and strong punishment, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The synergy of reputation and punishment mechanisms is key: when both are present and the environment is strict (high threshold, high punishment), cooperation dominates. When punishment is absent or the reputation threshold is low (lenient environment), defection dominates. The model demonstrates that the structure and targeting of punishment (via group reputation) can be critical for sustaining cooperation. However, the findings are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the presence and targeting of punishment (especially when combined with reputation mechanisms) can dramatically increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment targeted at low-reputation groups is likely to increase efficiency if cooperation and efficiency are closely linked. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, the direct quantitative effect on efficiency cannot be inferred. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment design and reputation interact to affect cooperation, which may be a proxy for efficiency in similar models.

- source: Quan, J., Yu, J. Y., Li, X., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Conditional switching between social excluders and loners promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion strategy with dynamic (state-dependent) costs into a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the average level of cooperation in the population. Higher probability of exclusion success and lower exclusion cost parameters both expand the parameter space where cooperation dominates. The Moore neighborhood structure (more neighbors per player) is more conducive to cooperation than the traditional neighborhood. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic-cost social exclusion (a form of punishment) can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially with high exclusion probability and low cost. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that, under similar game design dimensions, enabling effective punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The effect of neighborhood size (player count per group) is also highlighted: larger groups (Moore neighborhood) facilitate higher cooperation. For efficiency prediction, this paper provides indirect support that punishment can be beneficial, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Effects of interconnections among corruption, institutional punishment, and economic factors on the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Corruption (bribery) reduces the level of cooperation when cooperators are more inclined to bribe, undermining the effectiveness of institutional punishment. (2) Both stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) and richer economic potential (higher pool multiplier) enhance cooperation, but their effects interact with the bribery tendencies of group members. (3) When defectors are more likely to bribe, strong leaders can sustain cooperation under poor economic potential, but in rich economic environments, weaker leaders may be more beneficial. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and the stability of cooperation/defection equilibria, not on direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, corruption, and economic potential interact to affect cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment's effectiveness in promoting cooperation is reduced by corruption, especially when cooperators are more likely to bribe. Stronger punishment and higher economic potential generally expand the basin of attraction for cooperation, but the effect can reverse depending on who bribes more. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of corruption and the relative strength of punishment and economic potential are key moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation, but direct translation to efficiency outcomes requires caution.

- source: Du, K., Fan, R. G., Xue, H., Wang, Y. T., & Bao, X. G. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game based on heterogenous social preferences and dynamic punishment. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) heterogeneous social preferences (especially altruism) promote higher cooperation rates in spatial PGGs; (2) dynamic punishment mechanisms are more effective than static punishment in increasing cooperation and addressing second-order free-riding; (3) the enhancement factor (r_e, analogous to MPCR) is a key moderator—when it is low, cooperation is hard to sustain even with punishment, but when it is high, cooperation is high even without punishment; (4) inequality aversion slightly inhibits cooperation, and reciprocity has a mixed effect depending on r_e. All findings are based on simulation of agent-based models, with the main outcome being the proportion of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that enabling punishment (especially dynamic punishment) increases cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, particularly when the enhancement factor is moderate. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment and social preference heterogeneity affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings. The model's focus on spatial structure and dynamic punishment is relevant for games with similar features.

- source: Lazarus, J. (2023). Cooperation and social policy: integrating evidence into practice - Introduction to the Issue. *Global Discourse*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from experimental and real-world studies showing that a variety of factors—including financial incentives, reciprocity, fairness, reputation, group identification, and social norms—can increase cooperation in public goods games and similar social dilemmas. Punishment of free-riding and reward of cooperation are identified as effective mechanisms for increasing cooperation, especially when they are costly to the punisher/rewarder and when implemented by long-term partners. The review also notes that communication, group identity, and information about others' behavior can further enhance cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a high-level theoretical synthesis of factors that promote cooperation in public goods games, including the role of punishment. It is useful for identifying which game design dimensions are likely to influence cooperation rates, but it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially under conditions of group identification, communication, and when punishment is costly to the punisher. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits its value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design dimensions.

- source: Zachník, V. (2023). Social Norms and Agent Types: Bridging the Gap Between the Theoretical Models and Their Applications. *Philosophy of the Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes a refined theoretical model of social norms that distinguishes between norm subjects, enforcers, and audience, and analyzes their mutual influences and behavioral possibilities. It argues that the presence of enforcers (who punish norm violations) is crucial for sustaining cooperation, referencing public goods game experiments and the literature on punishment. The paper also discusses how group identity, audience effects, and the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can affect cooperation and norm stability. It introduces the concepts of 'misplacement' (enforcers avoiding punishment duties) and 'identification avoidance' (subjects avoiding norm-triggering situations) as subtle forms of norm violation. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes; its contribution is conceptual and integrative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insights into how punishment, enforcement roles, and group structure may affect cooperation and norm stability in public goods game-like environments. It affirms, based on cited literature, that punishment can increase cooperation and that the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can moderate these effects. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of agent roles, group identity, and enforcement structure as potential moderators, but it does not supply empirical data for model training or calibration.

- source: Levy, D. A. (2022). Optimizing the social utility of judicial punishment: An evolutionary biology and neuroscience perspective. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the human tendency to punish non-cooperators, even at a personal cost, is rooted in evolutionary and neural mechanisms. It reviews evidence from public goods games and other economic experiments showing that punishment can increase cooperation and, in some cases, average group profit. The author discusses the adaptive value of punishment, the neural reward mechanisms involved, and the importance of institutionalizing punishment to maintain social order and cooperation. The paper also highlights complexities and limitations, such as the costliness of punishment, the potential for retaliation, and the need for centralized enforcement in large societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games and, under some conditions, improve group outcomes. It references empirical studies (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, Gächter et al., 2008) that find punishment can increase average profit, but also notes limitations and boundary conditions (e.g., costliness, retaliation, institutional context). For the prediction task, this paper supports the general mechanism by which punishment can improve efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence or dimension-specific effect sizes. It is most useful for contextualizing why and when punishment might be effective, rather than for precise prediction.

- source: Frey, U. J., & Burgess, J. (2023). Why do climate change negotiations stall? Scientific evidence and solutions for some structural problems. *Global Discourse*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that climate change negotiations are structurally analogous to public goods games, with similar challenges of free-riding, group formation, and the need for institutional mechanisms to foster cooperation. It reviews experimental evidence showing that the possibility of sanctioning (punishment) increases cooperation and, over time, can improve group welfare, though some studies note that punishment can be inefficient in the short term. The authors argue that the absence of enforceable sanctions in climate agreements is a key reason for stalled progress and that introducing sanctions, improving transparency, and building trust and fairness are critical for better outcomes. However, the paper does not report new empirical data or direct efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases cooperation and, in the long run, can improve group welfare and efficiency, though it acknowledges that punishment can sometimes be inefficient in the short term. It highlights the importance of institutional design features such as sanctioning, transparency, and trust. However, because it does not report new empirical data or direct efficiency outcomes, it should be used as contextual and mechanistic support rather than as a source of quantitative effect sizes for efficiency prediction.

- source: Duong, M. H., Durbac, C. M., & Han, T. A. (2024). Cost Optimisation of Individual-Based Institutional Reward Incentives for Promoting Cooperation in Finite Populations. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the expected total cost to the institution for achieving a target cooperation level in finite-population PGGs depends strongly on the threshold for incentive application (t), the strength of selection (β), mutation rate (μ), and the type of incentive (reward, punishment, hybrid). For small thresholds (t=1), the cost function is always non-decreasing in the per-capita incentive cost, and explicit formulas are derived. For larger thresholds, the cost function can exhibit non-monotonic behavior and phase transitions, especially at high selection strength. Numerical results show that both reward and punishment incentives can be optimized for cost, but the optimal cost and its behavior depend on the game parameters and incentive scheme. The paper provides a framework for predicting the institutional cost required to achieve a desired cooperation level, given the game design and population parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence on how the cost of institutional incentives (reward, punishment, hybrid) required to achieve a target cooperation level in PGG-like environments depends on game design dimensions such as player count, group size, marginal per-capita return, and the structure of the incentive scheme (threshold t, cost per unit, effectiveness). While the main outcome is the institutional cost rather than group efficiency, the results are directly relevant for predicting how changes in punishment or reward design (e.g., cost, threshold, effectiveness) will affect the efficiency of institutional intervention. The paper is most informative for settings where the goal is to optimize the cost of achieving cooperation, rather than to maximize group payoff per se. It provides explicit formulas and numerical results that can be used to inform predictions about the efficiency of punishment or reward interventions, conditional on game design and baseline cooperation levels.

- source: Jia, D. Y., & Wang, X. F. (2024). Synergistic effects of global exclusion and mutation on replicator dynamics of public cooperation. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that global exclusion (a centralized exclusion mechanism) in the public goods game leads to higher group efficiency and can robustly promote cooperation, even in the presence of unbiased mutation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations show that, for reasonable exclusion costs, the stationary level of global excluders (and thus group payoff) is higher than in standard PGGs. Mutation (strategy exploration) can synergistically interact with global exclusion, further increasing the emergent level of cooperation and efficiency, with an optimal mutation rate maximizing this effect. The model also identifies bifurcation structures and regions of parameter space where global exclusion is most effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling a centralized exclusion mechanism (a form of punishment) in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency, especially when the cost of exclusion is not too high. The effect is robust to the presence of unbiased mutation (strategy exploration), and there is an optimal mutation rate that maximizes efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, given a control game without exclusion/punishment, enabling a global exclusion mechanism (with reasonable cost) will increase average efficiency, and that the effect is further enhanced by moderate levels of mutation/exploration. The results are most directly applicable to infinitely well-mixed, large-population, one-shot or non-repeated PGGs with centralized exclusion and no second-order free-riding. The model does not address peer punishment, reward, or repeated interactions.

- source: Wang, X. F., & Perc, M. (2022). Replicator dynamics of public goods games with global exclusion. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a global exclusion mechanism (a centralized institution that can exclude defectors from the public good) into the public goods game can lead to higher efficiency and average group payoff, provided the cost of exclusion is not too high and the probability of successful exclusion is sufficiently large. The model predicts that, in these parameter regions, cooperation can emerge and be stabilized, sometimes through cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and excluders. The results are robust across a wide range of functional forms for the exclusion probability. When exclusion is too costly or too unlikely, the system reverts to defection and low efficiency. The paper does not analyze empirical data but provides theoretical and numerical phase diagrams supporting these conclusions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games, the addition of a sufficiently effective and not overly costly global exclusion (institutional punishment) mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency compared to the baseline game. The effect is robust to the details of how exclusion is implemented, as long as the exclusion probability is high enough and the cost is not prohibitive. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, when the game design includes a global exclusion mechanism with moderate or low cost and high effectiveness, one should expect a significant increase in efficiency relative to the control. The model also clarifies that if exclusion is too costly or ineffective, efficiency gains will not materialize. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of institutional punishment/exclusion in PGG-like environments.

- source: Xie, K., & Liu, T. J. (2024). The regulation of good and evi promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to both punish defectors and reward cooperators (PEPG strategy) in a spatial public goods game, especially when combined with heterogeneous payoff allocation based on reputation, leads to higher cooperation rates and higher average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the enhancement factor (r), the importance of reputation in payoff allocation (a), and the strength of the bonus-penalty (gamma). Defectors' average payoffs are always lower than those of cooperators in the steady state. The model demonstrates that the union of PEPG and reputation-based heterogeneity is more effective than either alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides simulation evidence that combining punishment and reward (PEPG) with reputation-based heterogeneous payoffs increases cooperation and average payoffs in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward and reputation mechanisms) will increase efficiency, particularly when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr, via r), the effectiveness of punishment/reward (gamma), and the importance of reputation (a) are high. However, the model is not a direct empirical test and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative optimum, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Ibrahimli, R., Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2024). Migration costs and rewarding schemes in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study simulates spatial public goods games where agents can migrate between groups if unsatisfied. Four setups are compared: baseline (migration only), migration cost (punishment), rewarding scheme (loyalty reward), and combined. Migration costs (punishment) robustly increase average contribution, especially at low enhancement factors, and are more effective than rewards. The combined mechanism can further improve cooperation in some cases. The main reported outcome is average contribution, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that institutional punishment (here, migration cost) is a stronger driver of cooperation than institutional rewards in this spatial setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, migration costs) increase cooperation (contribution) more than rewards in spatial PGGs, especially at low enhancement factors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding how migration costs and rewards affect contribution rates under different enhancement factors, group sizes, and migration probabilities. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2022). The rise and fall of cooperation in populations with multiple groups. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of inequity-averse individuals (both punishers and rewarders) can promote the survival of contributors and the elimination of defectors in spatial public goods games, but the effectiveness depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost (alpha), and the intensity (beta) of punishment/reward. First-order inequity aversion (punishment) is more effective at low r, while second-order (reward) is more effective at high r. Increasing the cost of punishment/reward (alpha) undermines the effect of punishment more than reward. Increasing the intensity (beta) generally promotes cooperation, but can also lead to cyclic dominance phases where defectors persist. The model shows that the traditional result that punishment is more effective than reward does not always hold when punishment/reward is based on payoff inequity rather than strategy. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so implications for efficiency are indirect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and reward mechanisms, when based on payoff inequity rather than strategy, affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment and reward depends on the synergy factor, cost, and intensity, and that the relationship is non-monotonic and context-dependent. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most relevant for understanding how design dimensions like mpcr (r), punishment/reward cost and effectiveness, and the presence of reward interact to affect the prevalence of cooperation, but not directly the efficiency.

- source: Li, S. L., Du, C. P., Li, X. X., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2024). Antisocial peer exclusion does not eliminate the effectiveness of prosocial peer exclusion in structured populations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in structured populations, prosocial peer exclusion can uphold cooperation even in the presence of antisocial peer exclusion, except when exclusion costs are low. Antisocial peer exclusion alone suppresses cooperation, but when both types coexist, prosocial exclusion remains effective at sustaining cooperation through complex coexistence and cyclic dominance patterns. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates and strategy frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are robust across different network sizes and parameter sweeps, but are based on theoretical simulations rather than empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that prosocial peer exclusion can maintain high levels of cooperation in spatial public goods games, even when antisocial exclusion is possible, except at low exclusion costs. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates and not as efficiency or group payoff, the direct quantitative effect of exclusion on efficiency cannot be extracted. The results suggest that, in structured populations, enabling prosocial exclusion (a form of targeted sanctioning) is likely to increase cooperation rates, and that the negative impact of antisocial exclusion does not fully undermine this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support: higher cooperation rates likely correspond to higher efficiency, but the mapping is not explicit. The findings are most relevant for settings with spatial structure, peer-based exclusion, and variable exclusion costs.

- source: Wang, J. W., Dai, W. H., He, J. L., Yu, F. Y., & Shen, X. (2022). Persistent imitation paves the way for cooperation in public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of prior agreements and posterior compensation (punishment of defectors with compensation to cooperators) in a spatial PGG can promote cooperation, but the effect is highly non-linear and context-dependent. Increasing the cost of compensation (punishment) can lead to abrupt transitions in cooperation rates, and the effect of the agreement threshold is non-monotonic, with intermediate thresholds maximizing cooperation. Importantly, even without punishment (compensation), cooperation can persist within effective agreements due to clustering of conditional cooperators. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment-like mechanisms (posterior compensation) and agreement thresholds affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but does not report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment does not always increase cooperation (and by extension, may not always increase efficiency), and that the effect depends on the cost of punishment and the agreement threshold. The findings highlight the importance of non-linear and threshold effects, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes. The model is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and possible moderators of punishment effects, rather than for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Yang, L. H., Ning, Y. P., & Zhang, L. Z. (2025). Reconstruction of micro-dynamics characterizing human decision-making behavior in repeated social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishing the minority strategy in each group (regardless of whether it is cooperation or defection) can strongly promote the emergence of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the punishment fine increases, the fraction of cooperators rises, and for high punishment, the system can abruptly transition from full defection to full cooperation as the multiplication factor increases. Theoretical analysis shows that the payoff difference between cooperators and defectors increases with punishment severity, favoring the expansion of cooperator clusters. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of this punishment rule on overall welfare or efficiency remains unclear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishing the minority strategy in a spatial PGG can increase the fraction of cooperators, especially with higher punishment fines. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. Theoretical payoff differences are provided, but without explicit efficiency or welfare analysis, it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates to higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment. The paper is most informative for predicting cooperation rates under minority punishment rules, but less so for efficiency predictions required by the downstream task.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. Y., Chen, W. M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Reputation-dependent social learning on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a conditional strategy—where agents can switch between excluding defectors and opting out as loners—substantially increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. Lower tolerance for defectors (i.e., agents become loners more readily), lower exclusion costs, and higher exclusion probability all lead to higher cooperation levels. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperative strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report efficiency as a fraction of the social optimum, nor does it provide direct evidence on how these mechanisms affect group payoff or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional exclusion and voluntary opt-out mechanisms can increase the frequency of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when exclusion is cheap and effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs.
