For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (social exclusion) are more effective when costs are low and effectiveness is high, but the translation to efficiency outcomes remains uncertain.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, M. Y., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2023). Combined effect of pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that social exclusion is a more effective strategy than punishment for promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. Introducing antisocial punishment (defective punishers) helps eliminate second-order free-riding by cooperators who do not bear exclusion costs. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the exclusion probability, antisocial punishment fine, and exclusion/punishment costs. High exclusion probability and low antisocial punishment fine promote cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperative excluders can protect cooperators from invasion by defectors and defective punishers, and that the combination of social exclusion and antisocial punishment can sustain high cooperation levels. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms like social exclusion and antisocial punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, which may be associated with higher efficiency in practice. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how the structure and parameters of punishment and exclusion mechanisms affect cooperation dynamics, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in similar game designs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper's results should be treated as informative about behavioral mechanisms but not as direct evidence.

- source: Liu, L. J., Xiao, Z. L., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Early exclusion leads to cyclical cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, the ability to exclude defectors early (after monitoring) leads to cyclical dominance among cooperators, defectors, and excluders, and maintains high average cooperation rates. If exclusion is delayed, defectors can dominate. The model shows that the timing of exclusion, monitoring cost, and discount factor are key moderators. While the model defines payoffs and uses them in evolutionary dynamics, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that enabling a form of punishment (social exclusion) can sustain high cooperation rates if implemented early, and that the timing and cost of exclusion are critical moderators. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as informative-indirect evidence. The model's structure and parameterization (group size, rounds, exclusion cost, monitoring cost) can inform how similar design dimensions might affect the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated PGGs, but direct quantitative predictions about efficiency are not available.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Gavrilets, S., Qin, C. Z., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). Kinship can hinder cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods and multi-player PD games with heterogeneous cost-benefit ratios, increasing genetic relatedness does not always increase cooperation or efficiency. For low relatedness, direct reciprocity (punishment via GRIM) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency. For high relatedness, cooperation is a dominant strategy and efficiency is high. However, for intermediate relatedness, some individuals become unconditional cooperators (AllC), which undermines the effectiveness of punishment and allows others to defect (AllD), making full cooperation and maximal efficiency unsustainable. This results in a non-monotonic, U-shaped relationship between relatedness and efficiency. The findings are supported by both subgame perfect equilibrium analysis and agent-based evolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public goods games with heterogeneity in cost-benefit ratios, the effect of mechanisms analogous to punishment (i.e., direct reciprocity via GRIM) on efficiency depends critically on the population structure and relatedness. When relatedness is low, punishment can sustain high efficiency; when relatedness is high, cooperation is self-sustaining. However, at intermediate relatedness, the threat of punishment is undermined, and efficiency drops. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity and the degree of relatedness (or analogous parameters) can cause non-monotonic effects of punishment on efficiency, and that punishment is most effective at low relatedness. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated PGG-like games with heterogeneity, but less so for one-shot games or games with explicit costly punishment mechanisms.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Wang, T. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games under a risk-transfer mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a risk-transfer (coalition) mechanism into a spatial public goods game can enhance or reduce cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient (r) and the coalition closeness (alpha). In two-strategy systems, coalitional cooperators are more competitive than traditional cooperators, and coalitional defectors are less competitive than traditional defectors. In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Kristensen, N. P., Chisholm, R. A., & Ohtsuki, H. (2025). Many-strategy games in groups with relatives and the evolution of coordinated cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that kin selection (homophily) can facilitate the initial invasion of coordinated cooperation in public goods games with nonlinear (sigmoidal) benefits, even when such cooperation cannot invade in a well-mixed population due to cognitive or coordination costs. Once established, coordinated cooperation can persist as homophily declines, provided that the benefit function is sufficiently nonlinear and the switching gain from non-contribution to contribution at the coordination point exceeds the cost. If this condition is not met, some level of homophily must be maintained to prevent the invasion of defectors or 'liars' (who commit to contribute but do not follow through). The framework provides analytical and numerical tools for predicting evolutionary outcomes (including efficiency and group payoff) as a function of group size, relatedness, benefit function nonlinearity, and strategy set. The results are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but do not address punishment or sanctions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a general theoretical framework for predicting efficiency and group payoff in public goods games with arbitrary group size, number of strategies, and non-additive (e.g., sigmoidal) benefit functions, under varying levels of kin structure (homophily). It is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR (via the benefit function), and group structure. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The framework could, in principle, be extended to include punishment as a discrete strategy, but this is not done in the present paper. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting baseline efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but provides only indirect or contextual support for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Optional participation only provides a narrow scope for sustaining cooperation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, cooperation can only be sustained (and thus group efficiency improved) when nonparticipants have positive outside payoffs and do not harm the public good. The classic 'loner' model is a special case of this. If nonparticipants harm the public good or have negative outside payoffs, cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings highlight the limitations of bottom-up, voluntary participation schemes for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency and cooperation levels in optional participation (loner) variants of the public goods game, which are sometimes considered as alternatives to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control conditions where only optional participation is available, but not the effect of enabling punishment. It also clarifies that optional participation alone only supports cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) under narrow conditions.

- source: Dong, Y. T., He, Z. X., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Constructive agents nullify the ability of destructive agents to foster cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, destructive agents (who harm others at a cost to themselves) can induce cyclic dominance among strategies (cooperation, defection, destruction), which can sustain cooperation and thus increase group efficiency relative to the baseline. However, when constructive agents (who benefit others at a cost to themselves) are introduced, they do not by themselves promote cooperation or efficiency. If both types are present, constructive agents can nullify the ability of destructive agents to sustain cooperation if their benefit exceeds the harm from destructive agents. The efficiency outcome thus depends on the relative magnitude of constructive and destructive effects. The paper provides explicit replicator dynamics and invasion analyses to support these findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with additional positive or negative externalities (constructive or destructive agents), but only indirectly relevant for classic punishment-based games. The 'destructive agent' is adjacent to punishment: it is a non-participant who harms others, but not a targeted, strategic punishment mechanism. The efficiency effects of destructive agents are positive (via cyclic dominance) when their harm is moderate, but are nullified if constructive agents' benefits are larger. The findings suggest that the presence and magnitude of positive and negative externalities can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, because the model is theoretical and does not include classic peer punishment (where players pay to reduce the payoff of specific others in response to their actions), direct quantitative predictions for standard punishment treatments should be made with caution. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented as a non-strategic, non-targeted externality.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of spatial public goods games with insurance mechanisms. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that insurance mechanisms in spatial PGGs can both promote and inhibit cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient, insurance premium, coverage, and the structure of the population. At low synergy coefficients, insurance (especially when purchased by defectors) can sustain cooperation through self-organized cyclic states, increasing group efficiency relative to baseline defection. However, at high synergy coefficients or when insured cooperators are prevalent, insurance can hinder cooperation due to indirect territorial competition. When insurers are allowed to adapt their strategies (two-layer network), the cooperation-promoting effect of insurance is often reduced, as insurers may refuse to transact with defectors, limiting the spread of insurance and altering the evolutionary dynamics. The model provides detailed phase diagrams mapping these effects, with efficiency interpreted as the prevalence of cooperative strategies and the resulting group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how insurance-like risk management mechanisms (adjacent to punishment) affect efficiency in spatial PGGs. While not a direct study of punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games with sanctioning or risk-mitigation features. The results suggest that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to game parameters (e.g., synergy coefficient, insurance cost/benefit, network structure) and the ability of third parties (insurers) to adapt. For prediction tasks, the paper highlights the importance of considering not just the presence of a sanctioning or insurance mechanism, but also who can access it, its cost-effectiveness, and the possibility of coevolution with external agents. The evidence is most directly applicable to spatial, structured populations with risk-mitigation options, and less so to standard punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Wei, M., Wang, X., Liu, L. Z., Zheng, H. W., Jiang, Y. S., Hao, Y. J., Zheng, Z. M., Fu, F., & Tang, S. T. (2025). Indirect reciprocity in the public goods game with collective reputations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with collective reputation and indirect reciprocity, all of the leading-eight social norm strategies can sustain cooperation in social dilemmas when the group assessment criterion (social strictness) is set at a moderate level. Too relaxed or too strict assessment criteria undermine cooperation. The evolutionary success of different strategies and assessment criteria depends on the payoff environment: stricter criteria are favored in low-payoff (high-dilemma) environments, while relaxed criteria are favored as payoffs increase. The model shows that collective reputation can mitigate social dilemmas and that the interplay between game structure and reputation structure is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task by showing that, in public goods games with indirect reciprocity and collective reputation (but without explicit punishment), cooperation and expected payoffs are maximized at moderate levels of social strictness. The findings suggest that the structure of reputation assessment (a design dimension not in the standard list) can play a similar role to punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the absence of explicit punishment means the results cannot be directly transferred to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding how non-punitive, reputation-based mechanisms affect cooperation and payoffs, and for contextualizing the importance of social norm design. It does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2024). Evolutionary Dynamics of Preguidance Strategies in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper introduces and analyzes two forms of preemptive guidance strategies (peer and pool guidance) in a one-shot public goods game with a pregame negotiation phase. Both forms of guidance can reduce the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders (cooperators who do not guide). Pool guidance is more effective than peer guidance, especially at higher guidance costs. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff. The model does not include standard punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on group payoff or efficiency as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that preemptive guidance strategies can reduce free riding and increase the prevalence of guidance strategies in PGGs, especially when guidance costs are low. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The guidance strategies studied are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the dynamics of pre-commitment or preemptive intervention, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency outcomes from punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Battu, B. (2023). Co-evolution of conditional cooperation and social norm. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model finds that high initial cooperation and low contribution costs favor the emergence and stability of high cooperation and moderate norm sensitivity. When contribution costs are high or initial cooperation is low, cooperation fails to establish. The noise parameter (imperfect information about past cooperation) can modulate but not destroy cooperation unless it is very high. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, norm enforcement is internalized as psychic costs for free-riding, which depend on the number of cooperators, the cost of contribution, and individual norm sensitivity. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how internalized norm enforcement (psychic costs) and conditional cooperation can sustain cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially under low contribution costs and high initial cooperation. However, it does not model or report the effects of explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of initial conditions, contribution cost, and norm sensitivity for the emergence of cooperation, but not for quantifying efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hu, D., Liu, J. Z., Liu, C., & Chu, C. (2025). Dynamic incentives and environmental feedback in public goods games: Promoting cooperation through critical thresholds. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing dynamic, group-level environmental feedback (bonuses or penalties based on collective contribution thresholds) in a spatial public goods game strongly promotes cooperation, especially when the synergy factor is low. The effect is not due to individual punishment or reward, but to a group-level adjustment of payoffs. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies and incentives. The model shows that higher expectation thresholds for group contributions can, counterintuitively, make cooperation more likely, and that the system transitions from defection to cooperation as the dynamic incentive increases. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. Instead, it models a group-level dynamic incentive (environmental feedback) that is not targeted at individuals and is not costly to assign. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how group-level feedback mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The results are most relevant for adjacent mechanisms (dynamic group-level incentives) rather than direct punishment or sanctions.

- source: Sun, X. P., Zhu, H. R., Kang, H. W., Bi, Y. Z., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of memory reputation-induced tax and reward allocation on spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-driven mechanism for adjusting local synergy factors in a spatial public goods game can significantly promote cooperation. The effect is strongest when the maximum possible synergy factor is high, the minimum is low, the adjustment step size is small, and the reputation threshold is moderate. Both excessively high and low reputation thresholds hinder cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, reputation indirectly affects payoffs by modulating the local multiplier. The main reported outcome is the proportion of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment mechanism. However, it is relevant for understanding how endogenous, reputation-driven changes to the payoff structure (via the synergy factor) can affect cooperation rates. The findings suggest that indirect, reputation-based incentives can substitute for or complement explicit punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation, but the results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only adjacent, indirect evidence.

- source: Podder, S., & Righi, S. (2023). Complexity of Behavioural Strategies and Cooperation in the Optional Public Goods Game. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in the OPGG, increasing the complexity of behavioral strategies (i.e., the ability to condition actions on more finely-grained reputational information) increases the proportion of cooperative actions, especially under social norms that assign different reputations to defectors and loners. However, this effect plateaus after a certain level of complexity. Cooperation is higher in smaller groups, and the benefit of strategy complexity is most pronounced in these settings. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reputation-based mechanisms combined with complex behavioral strategies can sustain cooperation, but the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation systems and behavioral strategy complexity affect cooperation rates in OPGG settings without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, in the absence of punishment, increasing behavioral strategy complexity and using social norms that distinguish defectors from loners can increase cooperation rates, especially in small groups. However, it does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff, nor does it provide control or treatment efficiency values.

  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and modeling work on PGGs in disease and cancer, highlighting how cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on game parameters, network structure, and the presence of mechanisms like social norms or sanctions. It reviews how public health interventions (e.g., vaccination, mask mandates) can be conceptualized as public goods dilemmas, and how individual decision-making, social structure, and perceived costs/benefits shape compliance. In cancer, it discusses how cooperation among cancer cells can be modeled as a PGG, with implications for treatment strategies. However, the paper does not provide quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on how PGGs are used to model cooperation in health-related contexts and discusses mechanisms (including sanctions and incentives) that can promote cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform the qualitative understanding of how design dimensions (e.g., network structure, cost-benefit ratios, threshold effects) can influence cooperation, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency with or without punishment. - source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jin, Z. Y., Li, M. Y., Wang, M., Sun, X. P., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Discreet investors exert greater influence on cooperation in the public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of discreet investors—who pay a cost to obtain information about their neighbors' cooperation and adjust their investments accordingly—leads to higher average payoffs and more robust cooperation compared to standard cooperators, especially at low synergy factors (r). As the cost of information (C_DI) increases, the advantage of discreet investors diminishes, and at high synergy factors, standard cooperators can survive and dominate. The model does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcome is average payoff, which is a direct analog to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide evidence that mechanisms allowing players to condition their contributions on local information (at a cost) can increase average payoffs and cooperation, especially when the cost is low. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant because it does not manipulate or analyze punishment. It may be useful as a conceptual analog for the effects of costly information acquisition or heterogeneity in investment, but not for punishment effects.

- source: Park, C. (2022). Network Characteristic Control of Social Dilemmas in a Public Good Game: Numerical Simulation of Agent-Based Nonlinear Dynamics. *Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary PGGs, the average payoff and the prevalence of cooperation depend strongly on the participation cost, the incentive (interest rate), mutation rate, and the structure of the social network (network density, eigenvector centrality). High participation costs or low incentives lead to nonparticipation and lower average payoffs. High network density can mediate the effects of mutation, sometimes stabilizing cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not address the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or related environments. It is useful for understanding how network structure, mutation, and incentive parameters affect efficiency and cooperation in PGGs without punishment. It cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is modeled or analyzed.

- source: Wang, J. W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., Chen, W., Xu, W. S., Dai, W. H., & Ming, Y. X. (2023). Promotion, Disintegration and Remediation of group cooperation under heterogeneous distribution system based on peer rating. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Redistribution rules that reward willingness to cooperate (high alpha) and are neutral with respect to initial endowment (beta near zero) lead to the highest group efficiency (average payoff) and lowest inequality. Redistribution that favors the poor (negative beta) or the rich (positive beta) increases inequality and does not maximize group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning; all effects are due to redistribution of payoffs after the public goods game. The findings challenge the assumption that subsidizing the poor in such games always promotes cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it is highly informative about how redistribution mechanisms (distinct from punishment) affect efficiency and cooperation in heterogeneous populations. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it does provide a useful contrast: redistribution based on willingness to cooperate can increase efficiency, while redistribution based on endowment does not. The game design dimensions are well specified for redistribution, but not for punishment.

- source: Chessa, M., & Loiseau, P. (2024). Enhancing voluntary contributions in a public goods economy via a minimum individual contribution level. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that introducing a minimum individual contribution level (while still allowing for full free riding) in a voluntary public goods game can strictly increase the total contribution and social welfare at equilibrium, provided the minimum is not set too high. The analysis covers both heterogeneous and homogeneous cost cases, and characterizes the equilibrium structure as a function of the minimum. The results show that there is an optimal range for the minimum contribution: if set just above the lowest equilibrium contribution in the baseline game, it increases total contributions without causing contributors to drop out. If set too high, some contributors become free riders and total contributions can decrease. The paper provides operational guidance for setting the minimum but does not address punishment, reward, or other sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions. However, it is highly informative about the effects of restricting the contribution set by imposing a minimum, which is a different intervention. The findings may be useful for understanding how changes to the action space (such as minimums or defaults) affect efficiency, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., Bender, C., Langenbucher, K., Vollmeyer, R., & Schultze, M. (2024). Facets of altruism in study majors and volunteering groups. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that allowing heterogeneous investment and dynamic feedback on the multiplication factor in spatial public goods games can substantially increase both cooperation and group-level success (measured by average payoff and group success). High feedback strength and moderate group-preference/half-success effort are optimal for group success and individual welfare. The model recovers the standard spatial PGG when feedback is disabled. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are included or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for understanding how dynamic feedback and heterogeneous investment affect efficiency and payoff in public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. Its findings may be useful for predicting efficiency in PGGs with dynamic returns, but not for the specific prediction task of punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency. It provides no evidence on punishment, so should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a public goods game with environmental investment is promoted when social attention (driven by individual attitudes and environmental feedback) is high and when the investment budget exceeds a threshold. The model identifies three types of public response mechanisms to environmental degradation, with the 'precautionary' mechanism (rapid response to early degradation) being most beneficial for cooperation. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the parameters governing attention and forgetfulness, but the level of social attention itself is robust to these parameters within certain intervals. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are limited to the dynamics of cooperation and social attention in a PGG with environmental investment and evolving attitudes. It may provide indirect context for how non-punitive mechanisms (like attention or investment) affect cooperation, but is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yang, Z. Q. L., Li, C. C., Wang, X., & Tian, Y. L. (2025). PPO-ACT: Proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer for spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The PPO-ACT algorithm, which combines proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer, enables higher and more robust cooperation rates in spatial public goods games compared to standard PPO, Q-learning, and Fermi update rules. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no explicit analysis of group efficiency or total payoff is provided. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

Its findings are relevant for understanding how advanced learning algorithms affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions and results may be useful for modeling baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but not for the downstream task focused on punishment effects.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputation rule—where reputation is based on matching one's strategy to one's neighbors—combined with heterogeneous investment allocation (parameterized by α), strongly promotes cooperation in spatial PGGs. Higher heterogeneity (larger α) lowers the critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the region of parameter space where full cooperation is stable. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.

Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and heterogeneous investment can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Wang, S., Li, Z., Li, W. D., & Jiang, H. (2022). Continuous Spatial Public Goods Game Based on Particle Swarm Optimization with Memory Stability. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a continuous spatial PGG with PSO-based learning, the memory stability coefficient (α) has a complex effect on the average cooperation level. At low enhancement factors (r), high memory stability (large α) helps maintain a moderate level of cooperation, while reducing memory stability (lower α) leads to a collapse of cooperation. At high r, reducing memory stability (lower α) allows the system to forget low-investment strategies and promotes the formation of high-cooperation clusters, increasing the average cooperation level. The effect of memory stability is more pronounced when players rely more on their own history (high ω) than on neighbor imitation. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning rules and memory affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be indirectly useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial, continuous PGGs with PSO-type learning, but cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

  findings: The study finds that integrating neighbor-aware reinforcement learning (NI) and reputation mechanisms in a spatial public goods game model can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators and promote the formation of stable cooperator clusters. The effect is especially strong when agents can observe a wider neighborhood (larger perceptual range). Reputation alone can promote cooperation if it is sufficiently weighted in the reward function, but the combination of NI and reputation produces a synergistic effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms in the sense of costly peer or institutional sanctions/rewards. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning, reputation, and local information can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. The model and results may be useful for understanding moderators of cooperation in spatial and learning-based PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

However, all results are reported in terms of cooperator density, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. - source: Stock, E. V., Valverde, P., González-Avella, J. C., Iglesias, J. R., Gonçalves, S., & da Silva, R. (2025). Cyclical oscillations and absorbing-state probabilities in optional public goods games: Interplay of reward and group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the OPGG exhibits rich dynamical behavior, including cyclic dominance among strategies and multistability in absorbing states, depending on the multiplication factor (r) and group size (S). For r < 2, loners dominate; for r > S, cooperators dominate; and for 2 < r < S, the system can end in any of the three absorbing states, with the likelihood and duration of cycles depending on S and r. Larger group sizes and higher r values tend to favor cooperation, but the model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how group size and the multiplication factor affect the stability and dynamics of cooperation, defection, and abstention in optional public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment.

- source: Inaba, M., & Akiyama, E. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in multiplex networks through asymmetry between interaction and replacement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in multiplex network models of public goods games, the symmetry or asymmetry between the scope of interaction and the scope of strategy replacement can have non-monotonic effects on the evolution of cooperation. Specifically, in some cases, increasing asymmetry (e.g., local interaction with global replacement) can partially promote cooperation, contrary to previous findings that symmetry always promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is relevant for understanding how network structure and the symmetry of interaction and learning/replacement can affect cooperation rates. The findings may be useful for contextualizing the role of network and update rules in cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or sanctions.

- source: Battu, B., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Cooperation without punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation cannot be sustained by conditional cooperators alone, nor by simply injecting altruists if there is no social noise. However, when a moderate level of noise is present, a small but critical fraction of altruists can trigger and sustain high levels of cooperation among conditional cooperators. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and instead focuses on the role of positive reciprocity and social learning. The main outcome is the level of cooperation (contribution rate), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism in its model. Its findings are relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment for sustaining cooperation, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The paper does, however, argue that punishment can reduce social welfare, but this is a theoretical and literature-based claim, not a result of the presented model. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing context for non-punishment mechanisms, but not as a source of quantitative or comparative evidence on punishment's effect on efficiency.

The model replicates standard PGG dynamics, showing that cooperation rate increases with the synergy factor r, and that their synchronous update method can foster cooperation at lower r values compared to a baseline. However, the paper does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Chen, H., & Yang, J. (2022). Preferential selection and expected payoff drive cooperation in spatial voluntary public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial VPGG, introducing an expected payoff threshold and preferential selection in strategy updating can significantly increase the cooperation rate under certain parameter regimes. Specifically, positive expected payoff thresholds (p_e) and higher multiplication factors (r) promote cooperation, while the effect of the preferential selection parameter (a) depends on its sign and value. The cooperation rate is robust to a for positive p_e, but both a and r matter for negative p_e. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate; no efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how cooperation rates in a spatial VPGG are affected by expected payoff thresholds, preferential selection, and the multiplication factor. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually useful for understanding how non-punishment mechanisms (like aspiration levels and neighbor selection) affect cooperation rates.

- source: Xie, K., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Reputation in public goods cooperation under double Q-learning protocol. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that integrating reputation into both investment decisions and payoff calculations, especially when using double Q-learning, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The effect is strongest when the weight of reputation in payoff (η), the synergy factor (r), and the reputation sensitivity parameter (δ) are all sufficiently high. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even in challenging environments, but only when these parameters are jointly favorable. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution).

It is highly relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms and reinforcement learning protocols affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the lack of payoff-based outcome reporting, limit its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2025). Reinforcement learning in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both higher synergy coefficient (r) and stronger environmental feedback (alpha) promote higher levels of cooperation in the SPGG. The effect of the Q-learning discount factor (gamma) on cooperation is non-monotonic and depends on the values of r and alpha. When r or alpha is low, higher gamma (more future-oriented agents) promotes cooperation; when r and alpha are high, lower gamma (more myopic agents) is more favorable for cooperation. The results are based on agent-based simulations and focus on the fraction of cooperators and spatial clustering, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how environmental feedback and reinforcement learning affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or for the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions covered (e.g., player count, mpcr, rounds, absence of punishment/reward) may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation in spatial PGGs with environmental feedback, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

- source: Wang, X. Y., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2024). Conformity poses a double-edged sword effect on the evolution of cooperation within heterogeneous endowment populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the existence and maintenance of pure cooperator groups in a multi-group public goods game can promote the rise of cooperation in the population, even without mechanisms like punishment or reward. However, mutation and migration allow defectors to invade cooperator groups, undermining cooperation. Group division (splitting of large groups) can counteract the negative effects of mutation by generating new cooperator groups, but only if mutation rates are not too high and group division is sufficiently frequent. The synergy factor (r) and contribution level (c) also moderate these dynamics. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly transferable to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect context on how group structure, mutation, and migration affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., group division, migration) can strongly affect cooperation, and that the presence or absence of such mechanisms should be considered when interpreting or predicting the effects of punishment.

- source: Li, Y. P., Hu, X. Y., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., Yang, J. J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Environmental information perception enhances cooperation in stochastic public goods games via Q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a stochastic PGG with environmental feedback, the ability of individuals to perceive environmental state information significantly promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation. Specifically, the critical synergy factor (r) required for cooperation to emerge is lower when individuals can perceive the environment, compared to both a static environment and a dynamic environment that is not perceived. The study uses Q-learning agents on spatial networks and explores a variety of model extensions (update rules, neighborhood size, network topology, etc.), consistently finding that environmental information perception lowers the cooperation threshold. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms.

Its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that environmental information perception (not punishment) can promote cooperation (not efficiency) in stochastic PGGs. The results may be useful for understanding how information and feedback mechanisms affect cooperation thresholds, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., Zhou, S. Y., He, J. L., Xu, W. S., & Dai, W. H. (2024). The emergence of cooperation in the context of prior agreement with threshold and posterior compensation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game where only the top V individuals by cumulative wealth can participate and must pay a participation cost, the effect of participation cost on cooperation depends on V. For small V, increasing participation cost can promote cooperation up to a point but increases wealth polarization. For large V, increasing participation cost slightly inhibits cooperation but reduces polarization. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism studied.

- source: Flores, L. S., Vainstein, M. H., Fernandes, H. C. M., & Amaral, M. A. (2023). Heterogeneous contributions can jeopardize cooperation in the public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that in spatial PGGs with imitation dynamics, there is an optimal homogeneous contribution value that maximizes cooperation (measured as cooperator density) for a given lattice structure. Introducing heterogeneity in contributions (i.e., allowing cooperators to contribute different amounts) can reduce the overall level of cooperation compared to this optimum, due to parasitic interactions where higher-contribution cooperators are exploited by lower-contribution ones. The effect of heterogeneity depends on the lattice structure: on square lattices, heterogeneity is detrimental to cooperation at the optimum, while on triangular lattices (with clustering), the effect is less negative. The main dependent variable is the equilibrium density of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in contributions (a form of player heterogeneity) affects cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes. The results may be useful for contextualizing the impact of contribution heterogeneity as a moderator, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment. The absence of punishment means this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Alfaro, G., & Sanjuan, M. A. F. (2022). Time-dependent effects hinder cooperation on the public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

The multiplication factor (r) increases cooperation monotonically. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators in the population.

- source: Lu, Z. Y., Hua, S. J., Wang, L. C., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Hybrid reward-punishment in feedback-evolving game for common resource governance. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a feedback-evolving common resource game (a close variant of the public goods game), the introduction of punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) by a third-party institution significantly increases the likelihood of achieving both full cooperation and sustainable resource levels, which are direct analogs to high efficiency. Pure reward is less effective than punishment or hybrid schemes, especially when the resource growth rate is moderate. Increasing the per capita incentive (tax) and allocating more of the incentive budget to punishment (lower w) both make the ideal outcome (full cooperation and resource sustainability) more likely. When the resource growth rate is too low, no incentive scheme is effective. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by both analytical and numerical phase diagrams.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in environments where resource renewal is not too slow, adding punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) increases the likelihood of achieving high efficiency (full cooperation and resource sustainability) compared to reward alone or no incentives. The effect is stronger when the per capita incentive is higher and when more of the incentive budget is allocated to punishment. The model is deterministic and infinite-population, but the qualitative insights about the superiority of punishment (or hybrid) over pure reward for efficiency are likely to generalize to similar institutional public goods settings. The paper does not address stochasticity, finite groups, or experimental data, but its results are directly relevant for theoretical prediction.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). The paradigm of tax-reward and tax-punishment strategies in the advancement of public resource management dynamics. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both tax-funded punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation and sustain public resources, but only if the natural growth rate of the resource pool is sufficiently high. When the resource growth rate is low, even universal cooperation (driven by strong punishment or reward) cannot prevent resource depletion, so efficiency remains low. For moderate or high growth rates, the system can reach stable states with high cooperation and sustainable resources, but the tax-punishment model can exhibit bistability, meaning the final outcome depends on initial conditions.
