The magnitude of the tax (which determines the strength of punishment or reward) also affects whether the system stabilizes at full cooperation, coexistence, or defection. The models provide explicit, parameterized predictions for group efficiency and resource sustainability as a function of player count, tax size, and resource growth rate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment (and reward) on group efficiency and resource sustainability in public-goods-like resource management games. The models are not standard linear PGGs, but are close variants with dynamic, renewable resources and tax-based centralized punishment/reward. The findings show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by the natural growth rate of the resource pool and the magnitude of the tax (punishment strength). For low growth rates, punishment does not improve efficiency; for moderate/high growth rates, punishment can improve efficiency, but outcomes may be bistable. The results are most applicable to settings where resource dynamics and centralized sanctioning are key features. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions in such environments.

- source: Garrido, C. M., Santos, F. C., Domingos, E. F., Nunes, A. M., & Pacheco, J. M. (2025). A new electoral bottom-up model of institutional governance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) modeled as a threshold public goods game, the introduction of local, bottom-up institutions—created when a minimum number of group members contribute to their formation, and whose nature (reward or punishment) is decided by majority vote—substantially increases group efficiency (η_G) compared to no institution, reward-only, or punishment-only institutions. Reward-only institutions are generally more efficient than punishment-only, but the electoral (mixed) institution is superior to both, especially at lower risk levels. The model shows that the critical risk threshold for achieving a given level of group efficiency is lower under the electoral institution, and the transition from defection to cooperation is steeper. The model is theoretical and does not use empirical or experimental data, but provides direct predictions for how institutional design affects efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods games with risk of group failure, the presence and design of institutional punishment and/or reward mechanisms have large effects on group efficiency. Specifically, enabling local, flexible institutions that can implement either punishment or reward (as decided by group vote) leads to higher efficiency than fixed punishment-only or reward-only institutions. The model quantifies these effects using the η_G metric, which is directly analogous to efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a flexible, locally decided institution (with both punishment and reward possible) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than either punishment-only or reward-only, and much higher than no institution. The model also provides parameterizations for group size, number of rounds, risk, and institutional costs, which can inform predictions when these dimensions are known.

- source: Dughera, S. (2022). The evolution of workplace control leadership, obedience and organizational performance. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model finds that punishment (authoritarian leadership) can increase organizational performance relative to no control when workers are low-skilled or when monitoring is efficient, but is always less efficient than motivation (authoritative leadership) when workers are high-skilled and supervisors are charismatic. Multiple equilibria are possible: (1) 'Discipline' (universal cooperation with punishment), (2) 'Cooperation' (universal cooperation with motivation), (3) 'Conflict' (universal defection with punishment), and (4) mixed or cyclical regimes. The most efficient outcome is universal cooperation with motivation, but this requires high worker skill and supervisor charisma. Punishment can prevent the spread of shirking (the 'bad company' effect) by increasing procedural justice, but also reduces morale and is less efficient. Inefficient equilibria with widespread punishment and/or shirking can persist due to path dependence. The model provides explicit conditions for when each regime is stable and analyzes the welfare properties of each.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in a public-goods-like team production setting, conditional on game design dimensions such as monitoring efficiency (punishment tech), team size (player count), and the presence/absence of reward (motivation/charisma). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency relative to no control when monitoring is effective and workers are not highly skilled, but is always less efficient than a well-functioning reward/motivation regime. The model gives explicit parameter boundaries for when punishment is effective, and highlights the importance of initial conditions and path dependence. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to no-punishment in some parameter regimes (especially with low worker skill and high monitoring efficiency), but that the maximum efficiency is achieved with motivation/charisma rather than punishment. The model is highly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings where reward/motivation is not available or not effective.

- source: Vinayak, R. K. (2025). FARMER'S GAME: A GAME THEORETIC APPROACH TOWARDS BURNING OF CROP RESIDUE. *Journal of Dynamics and Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, without intervention, individual incentives lead to inefficient outcomes (universal burning of crop residue), analogous to the tragedy of the commons. Social pressure can sometimes induce cooperation, but is often not strong enough. The introduction of government punishment (penalty per burned plot) and/or reward (per non-burned plot) can shift the Nash equilibrium to full cooperation (no burning), maximizing group payoff and efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions for when this shift occurs: when the sum of penalty and reward per plot exceeds the net private benefit of burning minus the greener alternative and pollution cost. Simulations confirm these theoretical results, showing that moderate levels of punishment and reward are sufficient to achieve efficient, cooperative outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that introducing punishment (and/or reward) in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (all defect) to efficient (all cooperate), provided the policy parameters are set so that the penalty plus reward per unit exceeds the net private benefit of defection. The model is directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency: if the punishment is sufficiently strong (relative to the private incentive to defect), efficiency will increase dramatically, potentially reaching full efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment is implemented as a per-action cost, is known to all, and is not so severe as to cause practical or political infeasibility. The model does not address empirical implementation issues, but provides clear guidance on the direction and mechanism of efficiency change.

- source: Wang, L. C., Hua, S. J., Liu, Y. Y., Zhang, L., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Threshold incentive mechanisms for the sustainable management of public resources. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that threshold-based punishment and reward mechanisms can effectively promote cooperation and sustainable management of public resources, leading to higher efficiency (resource sustainability and group payoff) at lower cost than continuous incentives. Punishment is only triggered when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold, targeting defectors, while reward is triggered when cooperation is low or resources are depleted. These mechanisms can stabilize the system at higher resource levels and higher cooperation rates, but their effectiveness is limited if the resource's natural growth rate is too low or the initial resource stock is too small. The models also show that increasing population size can reduce equilibrium resource levels, and that the interplay of reward/punishment thresholds and strengths is important for optimal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that threshold-triggered punishment (and reward) mechanisms in public-goods-like resource games can increase efficiency relative to no incentives, especially when the resource can regenerate and initial conditions are not too dire. The effect of punishment is more positive for efficiency when it is targeted (threshold-based) rather than universal, and when combined with reward mechanisms. The model's explicit tracking of average payoffs and resource levels makes its results directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with similar design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all real-world behavioral nuances.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Shen, A. Z. (2024). The synergy of elimination and zero-determinant strategy on dynamic games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing endogenous reward and punishment policies in a regional public goods game increases both cooperation and total social welfare (W_t), especially when the intervention is targeted (partial intervention) rather than universal. The efficiency gains from punishment are largest when the cost of policy implementation is low or the punishment is strong. There is often an optimal fraction of groups to intervene in, beyond which additional intervention yields diminishing or negative returns due to policy costs. Size-based selection of groups for intervention is generally superior to random or degree-based selection. Allowing non-intervened groups to spontaneously adopt the policy can further boost cooperation and welfare, but only if the additional costs are offset by increased benefits. Terminating intervention leads to rapid collapse of cooperation and welfare, indicating the need for sustained policy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency (total social welfare), but the magnitude and even the sign of the effect depend on the cost of policy implementation, the strength of punishment, and the fraction of groups covered. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency relative to control, especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is not too low and policy costs are not too high. However, if policy costs are high or the environment is resource-poor (low mpcr), the efficiency gain may be small or even negative. The method of targeting intervention (e.g., size-based) can further moderate the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG variants with spatial or regional structure and endogenous institutional punishment.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2024). Impact of dynamic compensation with resource feedback on the common pool resource game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) static punishment (compensation) above a critical threshold leads to full cooperation, resource sustainability, and maximum group payoff; (2) dynamic punishment with resource feedback can promote cooperation and efficiency even when static punishment is insufficient, especially at low initial compensation intensity; (3) the relationship between punishment parameters and efficiency is non-monotonic and context-dependent, with oscillatory or convergent dynamics depending on parameter regions; (4) the proportion of fines used for ecological compensation (vs. other uses) should be tuned to maximize efficiency, with all fines used for compensation when feedback is high or initial intensity is high, and none when both are low.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing punishment intensity (compensation) above a critical threshold sharply increases efficiency (group payoff) in common pool resource games. Dynamic, feedback-based punishment can further improve efficiency, especially when static punishment is weak. The results suggest that, for prediction, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as whether it is dynamically adjusted based on resource feedback, are key moderators of efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with resource feedback, variable punishment intensity, and allocation of fines to resource restoration.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Governance of risky public goods under the threat of ostracism. *Physica D-nonlinear Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) model, local exclusion (ostracism) is more effective than local punishment in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target), especially at low risk levels. Local sanctioning institutions (both punishment and exclusion) are more effective than global ones. The advantage of exclusion over punishment is most pronounced when the risk of collective failure is low. When risk is high, both exclusion and punishment lead to high group achievement. The results are based on theoretical analysis and simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not on direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but group achievement is a closely related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods environments with risk of collective loss (CRD), enabling local punishment or exclusion mechanisms increases group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to no sanctions, with local exclusion (ostracism) being especially effective at low risk. The results suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment (especially local, exclusionary forms) is likely to increase efficiency or group success, particularly when the risk of failure is not too high. However, the findings are based on group achievement rather than explicit efficiency ratios, and are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data. The model parameters (e.g., group size, cost of exclusion, risk level) are specified, allowing some mapping to prediction dimensions.

- source: Esmaeili, P., Makui, A., Seyedhosseini, S. M., & Ghousi, R. (2022). The effect of probabilistic incentives to promote cooperation during the pandemics using simulation of multi-agent evolutionary game. *International Journal of Industrial Engineering Computations*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic third-party incentives (reward and punishment) can trigger abrupt transitions from low to high cooperation in a networked volunteer dilemma game. Pure punishment is more effective than pure reward, but a combination of both (with a higher share for reward) is optimal for promoting cooperation at lower monitoring costs. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations and catastrophe theory analysis, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and probability of punishment (and reward) can cause sharp increases in cooperation rates in volunteer dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can be a powerful lever for increasing cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar public goods settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of incentive mechanisms and the potential for nonlinear effects, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Yan, R. Y., Xu, M. Q., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Evolution of cooperation under pure institutional reward and punishment in collective-risk social dilemma games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing institutional pure punishment or pure reward into a collective-risk social dilemma game can promote cooperation, but with important differences. Pure reward leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, with the domain of coexistence expanding as reward intensity increases. Pure punishment produces more complex dynamics, including monostable, bistable, tristable, and quadristable outcomes, and can more effectively suppress free-riding when punishment intensity is high or the implementation threshold is low. In finite populations, lower thresholds for implementing incentives (reward or punishment) promote cooperation, while higher thresholds favor defection. The cost and intensity of incentives modulate these effects. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the evolutionary stability and prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of institutional punishment on cooperation in collective-risk public goods games, especially regarding how punishment intensity, cost, and implementation threshold affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on group efficiency or total payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under certain punishment regimes) are likely to be associated with higher efficiency, assuming the mapping from cooperation to efficiency is known. The results are most relevant for settings with institutional (not peer) punishment, nonlinear threshold public goods, and no direct cost of cooperation for punishers/rewarders. The findings suggest that lower punishment thresholds and higher punishment intensity (relative to cost) are more likely to promote cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if cooperation is strongly linked to group payoff.

- source: Wang, Z., Li, R. D., Jin, X., & Ding, H. (2023). Emergence of Social Norms in Metanorms Game With High-Order Interaction Topology. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in metanorm games on uniform random hypergraphs (URH), increasing group size (G) promotes the emergence of social norms, leading to higher cooperation rates and more agents willing to punish defectors. Network connectivity (number of groups) does not affect norm emergence. On heterogeneous hypergraphs (HRH), standard BV-learning fails to establish norms for 'leaver' agents, but a new dynamic relevance BV-learning algorithm enables both hub and leaver agents to establish norms and cooperate. Real-world hypergraph data (coauthorship networks) show similar patterns. The main findings are about the conditions for norm emergence and high cooperation, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-like games with high-order (group) interactions, especially regarding the role of group size and network structure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that larger group size and effective learning algorithms for punishment/metapunishment can promote cooperation and norm compliance, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, W., Zhao, D. K., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., An, T. B., Cui, G. H., & Wang, Z. (2025). Q-learning facilitates norm emergence in metanorm game model with topological structures. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a metanorm game with punishment and metapunishment, agents using self-regarding Q-learning can achieve high and robust cooperation rates (over 90% in many cases), even when agents lack information about others' payoffs. Punishment and metapunishment are essential for maintaining cooperation and suppressing defection and non-punishment. The model is robust to changes in network topology (scale-free, random, small-world), temptation payoff, population size, and observability. Highly connected agents are more likely to adopt punitive strategies. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this work.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and metapunishment mechanisms can sustain high cooperation rates in social dilemma environments with local interactions and limited information, especially when agents use reinforcement learning. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral and dynamic effects of punishment and metapunishment, and for anticipating that high cooperation is possible under a wide range of design parameters, but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2024). Coevolution of norm psychology and cooperation through exapted conformity. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can evolve in collective action dilemmas when injunctive norms strongly encourage punishment, but not when they only encourage cooperation. Conformity (descriptive norm psychology) alone can support intermediate cooperation, but is unstable and easily undermined. When conformity is exapted (pre-existing), it can expand the conditions under which punishment-based cooperation evolves, acting as a scaffold for the emergence of stable, high-cooperation societies. The model identifies two stable types of cooperative societies: those based on conformity (unstable, lower cooperation) and those based on punishment (stable, higher cooperation). However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of punishment norms (and the cost/benefit structure of punishment) in enabling high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when conformity is present. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective than conformity at sustaining high cooperation, and that the presence of conformity can expand the parameter space where punishment-based cooperation emerges. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment mechanisms (especially when normatively encouraged) are likely to increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cheng, H. H., Meng, X. Z., Hayat, T., & Hobiny, A. (2023). Multistability and bifurcation analysis for a three-strategy game system with public goods feedback and discrete delays. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can promote the survival of cooperators and the abundance of public goods, but only when their intensities are sufficiently high. Moderate reward and severe punishment are most conducive to cooperation. Excessively high reward does not always further increase cooperation. The cost of reward has a stronger negative effect on cooperation than the cost of punishment. Initial conditions and time delays can lead to multistability, oscillations, or extinction of cooperation. Large delays are detrimental to cooperation and can lead to the extinction of cooperators. The model highlights the complex interplay between feedback, incentives, and dynamic stability in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and public good abundance is positive when punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly, especially when combined with moderate reward. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as supporting the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and public good provision under certain parameter regimes, but not as direct evidence for efficiency gains. The results suggest that the effect of punishment is sensitive to the costs of incentives, the presence of reward, and dynamic factors such as delays and initial conditions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment can improve group outcomes in public-goods-like settings, but the mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Zhang, T. D., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Authority or Autonomy? Exploring Interactions between Central and Peer Punishments in Risk-Resistant Scenarios. *Entropy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that both central and peer punishment can promote group collaboration in risk-resistant (threshold) scenarios, with peer punishment generally having a stronger effect, especially as risk increases. Central punishment can suppress the effect of peer punishment at all risk levels, while peer punishment only constrains central punishment at high risk. Resource wastage from punishment is generally small compared to the loss from group failure, but increases with peer punishment intensity and risk. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in the standard sense, focusing instead on collaboration rates and resource wastage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that punishment (both central and peer) increases group collaboration in threshold public goods-like environments, and that the effectiveness and interaction of punishment types depend on risk level. However, because the main outcome is collaboration rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the model is theoretical/simulation-based rather than empirical, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The resource wastage analysis suggests that high peer punishment can lead to diminishing returns or inefficiency at high risk. The model's all-or-nothing threshold structure and explicit manipulation of punishment cost and effectiveness are informative for mapping to game design dimensions.

- source: Chica, M., & Santos, F. C. (2023). Seeding leading cooperators and institutions in networked climate dilemmas. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in networked collective-risk dilemmas with local punishment institutions, seeding a small fraction of punishers (institution supporters) at random or in network hubs can shift the system from low to high group achievement and institution prevalence, even when initial conditions are otherwise unchanged. Seeding cooperators is much less effective. The presence of punishment institutions is necessary for high cooperation; network structure alone does not suffice. Seeding network hubs is more expensive but not more effective than random seeding. The main outcomes are group achievement and institution prevalence, not direct efficiency or payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment institutions (via seeding punishers) in a networked public-goods-like environment can strongly increase group-level cooperation outcomes (group achievement and institution prevalence), even when the control (no punishment) condition yields low cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, its findings should be used as informative but indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, in similar networked games, enabling punishment (especially via institutional mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency if group achievement is a good proxy for efficiency. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on both network structure and the presence of institutional mechanisms, and that seeding punishers is more effective than seeding cooperators.

- source: Sarkar, S. (2023). Managing ecological thresholds of a risky commons. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled replicator-resource model with inspection and punishment, showing that punishment and monitoring can avert the tragedy of the commons (TOC) and sustain cooperation and resource abundance, but only under certain conditions. At low resource growth rates, even full cooperation (driven by punishment) cannot prevent resource collapse. At moderate growth rates, increasing punishment and monitoring can shift the system from oscillatory TOC to sustainable coexistence. At high growth rates, the resource persists even with high defection, but cooperation is harder to sustain. The model highlights the importance of ecological parameters (growth rate, predation) in moderating the effectiveness of punishment and monitoring. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in the sense of total group earnings or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and resource abundance depends on ecological parameters (especially resource growth rate and predation) as well as institutional parameters (punishment severity and monitoring probability). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment can prevent resource collapse or sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments with ecological feedbacks. The model structure and qualitative findings may inform priors about when punishment is likely to be effective, but quantitative efficiency predictions are not possible from this paper.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., Liang, S., Li, L., & Song, Y. J. (2024). Inter-layer feedback mechanism with reinforcement learning boosts the evolution of cooperation in multilayer network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that adaptive punishment mechanisms, when combined with reinforcement learning in hypergraph-structured PGGs, substantially increase the fraction of cooperators. Key findings include: (1) higher punishment probability and intensity promote cooperation, (2) moderate punishment costs are optimal for sustaining cooperation, (3) smaller group sizes (hyperedges) facilitate earlier and higher cooperation, and (4) higher learning rates and discount factors in Q-learning further enhance cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adaptive punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is likely, strong, and not too costly, and when group sizes are small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting how design dimensions (punishment cost, intensity, group size, etc.) affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency but are not equivalent. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect, mechanistic support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperation, but not direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2025). Picking strategies in games of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the strategy set and the microeconomic assumptions in evolutionary models of cooperation (including public goods games with punishment) can have a large impact on the predicted prevalence of cooperation. For example, allowing for antisocial punishment or for loners to be punished can lead to the collapse of cooperation, while more restrictive strategy sets can support high levels of cooperation. The paper emphasizes that these modeling choices can bias results and that more systematic, unbiased inclusion of strategies is needed for robust predictions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the abundance of cooperation and the evolutionary stability of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical caution for interpreting the effects of punishment in public goods games: the predicted effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) can depend strongly on the details of the strategy set and microeconomic assumptions. If a model or experiment restricts who can punish or be punished, or omits antisocial punishment, it may overestimate the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct quantitative support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of modeling assumptions and the potential for qualitative shifts in cooperation prevalence, which may translate to efficiency in some settings.

- source: Wu, J. H., Luan, S. H., & Raihani, N. (2022). Reward, punishment, and prosocial behavior: Recent developments and implications. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The review finds that both reward and punishment can promote prosocial behavior in public goods games and related social dilemmas, but their effectiveness depends on context. Punishment can sometimes reduce group welfare, especially when it leads to retaliation or is applied under noisy conditions, where it may mistakenly target cooperators and harm group payoffs. Reward is often found to be at least as effective as punishment in promoting cooperation and may be more efficient, particularly under noise. The legitimacy and source of punishment (e.g., third-party or democratic punishment) can moderate its effectiveness. Theoretical models suggest that reward is important for establishing prosociality, while punishment helps maintain it once established, but this dynamic has not been directly tested in experiments. The review also notes that indirect strategies like gossip may be more efficient than punishment in some real-world settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides useful qualitative guidance for predicting the effects of punishment and reward on prosocial behavior and, to a lesser extent, on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that punishment does not always increase efficiency and can sometimes reduce it, especially under noise or when perceived as illegitimate. Reward may be more efficient in some contexts, particularly when monitoring is imperfect. The review also highlights the importance of the structure and legitimacy of punishment mechanisms. However, because the review does not report quantitative efficiency outcomes or systematically analyze game design dimensions, its value for direct prediction of treatment efficiency is limited to qualitative boundary conditions and theoretical expectations.

- source: Itao, K., & Kaneko, K. (2025). Self-organized institutions in evolutionary dynamical-systems games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a minimal two-player harvesting game with environmental feedback, the evolution of decision-making functions leads to the self-organization of social institutions (shared norms and punishment rules). Players evolve to monitor both their own and their opponent's states, and punish selfish overharvesting by retaliatory overharvesting, which degrades the environment and reduces both players' payoffs. This punitive mechanism stabilizes cooperation and leads to the emergence of efficient, periodic harvesting patterns (modes) that maximize group fitness. The model shows that the most efficient (highest fitness) cooperative modes are only evolutionarily stable if players' strategies are sufficiently plastic to punish defectors. The results are robust across a range of parameter values. The paper provides a formal framework for understanding how punishment and norm evolution can sustain high efficiency in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (here, context-dependent punitive overharvesting) in a repeated common-pool resource game will increase group efficiency, provided that players can monitor each other's states or actions. The model shows that the emergence of punishment mechanisms is necessary for the stability of efficient cooperative outcomes, and that the efficiency gains are robust to parameter changes. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, binary-action resource games with environmental feedback, but the underlying principles are likely to generalize to other public goods or common-pool resource games with similar structure. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment, monitoring, and plasticity in strategies are key moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Cooperation and resource sustainability in coupling social-ecological systems with dynamic growth rates. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that a peer rating-based heterogeneous distribution system (PRHDS) can promote cooperation in a structured population, but is fragile to random exploration (strategy noise). The main threat is the invasion of egoistic cooperators, which undermines prosocial clusters. Introducing monetary punishments for minority raters (either as a fixed fine or as a reduction in distribution share) can restore and stabilize cooperation, making the system robust to low levels of noise. Non-monetary punishment (reducing the voice of minority raters) is ineffective. The effect of the system depends on the matching of the public goods multiplier and the weight of ratings in the distribution formula. The model is all-or-nothing in both contribution and rating, and the main reported outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates, strategy fractions), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a close variant of the public goods game with a peer rating-based distribution system, which acts as a combined reward/punishment mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, the results suggest that monetary punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs or share reductions) can stabilize cooperation in noisy environments, while non-monetary punishments are ineffective. The model is all-or-nothing, with no chat, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a key parameter. The findings suggest that, in similar designs, enabling monetary punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, efficiency), especially when the system is subject to noise. The absence of direct efficiency outcomes limits the strength of inference for the prediction task.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2024). Social norm dynamics and cooperation in changing groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that group changes, such as the entry of newcomers, tend to reduce cooperation in the short term because newcomers contribute less than incumbents. Over time, newcomers adapt to the incumbents' norms and increase their cooperation. Punishment mechanisms can induce newcomers to conform to group norms, but the review does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The evidence is based on both observational data from an online game (Ikariam) and lab experiments with public goods games and peer punishment. The review emphasizes the need for more causal and longitudinal research, especially on the role of normative differences and the potential for two-way adaptation between newcomers and incumbents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by summarizing how group composition changes and punishment mechanisms affect cooperation and norm conformity in public goods game-like environments. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited. The review suggests that punishment can help align newcomer behavior with group norms, which may support higher cooperation, but the lack of payoff data means that efficiency effects must be inferred indirectly from behavioral outcomes. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group dynamics interact, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Chiba-Okabe, H., & Plotkin, J. B. (2024). Can institutions foster cooperation by wealth redistribution?. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) A prosocial institution (maximizing average population payoff) can increase cooperation and efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently forward-looking (low discount rate); if it is myopic, it fails to intervene and the outcome is the same as anarchy. (2) A pro-cooperator institution (maximizing cooperator payoff) always increases cooperation and efficiency above anarchy, even if it is myopic. (3) A selfish institution (maximizing its own revenue) can sometimes increase efficiency above anarchy if it is forward-looking, but if it is myopic, it exploits the population and reduces efficiency below anarchy. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for when each regime leads to higher or lower efficiency than the no-institution baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of institutional interventions (taxation and redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the effect of 'punishment' or institutional intervention is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; rather, it depends on the institution's objective and its ability to value future outcomes (discount rate). For prediction tasks, the model suggests that institutional efficiency (analogous to punishment/reward effectiveness), the institution's foresight, and the alignment of institutional objectives with group welfare are critical moderators. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented as a tax-and-redistribute mechanism, rather than direct costly punishment between individuals.

- source: Pal, S., & Hilbe, C. (2022). Reputation effects drive the joint evolution of cooperation and social rewarding. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputation effects are crucial for the evolution of cooperation and social rewarding in PGG-like environments. When individuals can observe or infer others' rewarding strategies (i.e., reputation is possible), social rewarding and cooperation co-evolve and are stable, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. Without reputation, cooperation and rewarding do not emerge, and antisocial rewarding may arise under certain conditions (e.g., high assortment or mutually beneficial rewards). In multiplayer (PGG) settings, similar results hold: reputation enables stable cooperation and social rewarding, while its absence leads to defection and low efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and rewarding are payoff-maximizing, and shows that the presence of reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling reputation effects (e.g., making rewarding behavior observable) in PGG-like games will increase efficiency when social rewarding is possible. The model directly links game design dimensions such as group size, marginal per-capita return, reward cost and effectiveness, and information structure (reputation) to the emergence of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of observable rewarding (reputation), sufficiently high reward effectiveness relative to cost, and the ability to target rewards to cooperators are critical for efficiency gains from reward mechanisms. The absence of reputation or the presence of antisocial rewarding can undermine efficiency. While the paper does not model punishment directly, it provides indirect evidence that the structure and observability of incentives (including punishment, by analogy) are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Sugaya, T., & Wolitzky, A. (2023). Bad apples in symmetric repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in large repeated public goods or similar games, the possibility of sustaining cooperation and high efficiency depends critically on the ability to punish defectors specifically. If the only available incentives are rewards or withdrawal of provision (i.e., voluntary mechanisms), then cooperation collapses and efficiency is low when there are even a few commitment types (bad apples) who always defect. Involuntary punishment (such as fines) that can be targeted at defectors restores the possibility of cooperation and high efficiency. The main technical result is that if a 'population dominant' action exists (i.e., defecting yields a higher payoff than the population average, regardless of others' actions), then equilibrium efficiency converges to the inefficient outcome as the population grows. If no such action exists, and punishment can be targeted, then high efficiency is possible. The results are general and apply to a wide class of symmetric repeated games, including public goods games with or without transfers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods and related games. It implies that enabling targeted, involuntary punishment (e.g., fines for non-contributors) is necessary for sustaining high efficiency in large groups with possible commitment types. If only rewards or voluntary mechanisms are available, efficiency will be low in the presence of even a small fraction of defectors. The paper's results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is symmetric, the population is large, and there is a possibility of commitment types. For the prediction task, the presence or absence of targeted punishment (punishment_tech), the nature of the incentive (reward_exists, punishment_cost), and the ability to distinguish defectors are critical moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) will be low in large groups with commitment types unless involuntary punishment is available; enabling such punishment can dramatically increase efficiency.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Ember, C. R., Gelfand, M. J., Jones, E. C., Skoggard, I., Toutee, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2024). Cultural tightness and resilience against environmental shocks in nonindustrial societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that group efficiency (average payoff) and cooperation are maximized at intermediate levels of cultural tightness when facing severe environmental shocks. Loose cultures (low tightness) are more resilient and efficient under mild, slow-onset shocks, while very tight cultures often fail to adapt to new shocks and experience lower efficiency. However, very tight cultures can maintain high efficiency in the face of regular, severe shocks if cooperative norms are already established. Increasing group size undermines cooperation and efficiency unless benefit parameters scale accordingly. Synergistic effects between different types of collective action (public goods) enhance both cooperation and efficiency. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning, but norm compliance is enforced via conformity in the utility function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with norm-based conformity (but no explicit punishment), efficiency is maximized at intermediate levels of norm strength (tightness) under severe shocks, and at low tightness under mild shocks. The absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled, but the findings are highly informative for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, benefit structure, and norm strength. The model's structure and results are most relevant for public goods games with strong norm compliance or conformity-based incentives, and less so for games where punishment is the main enforcement mechanism. The model also shows that increasing group size reduces efficiency unless benefits scale up, and that synergy between collective actions increases the range of conditions for high efficiency.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics. The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., & Rand, D. G. (2022). Human Cooperation and the Crises of Climate Change, COVID-19, and Misinformation. *Annual Review of Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The review synthesizes evidence that mechanisms such as reputation, reciprocity, and social preferences (including egalitarianism and parochialism) are central to sustaining cooperation in social dilemmas. It highlights that in repeated public goods games, cooperation can be stabilized by making contributions observable and by enabling accountability through punishment or gossip. The review notes that punishment can promote cooperation, but also that gossip may be as effective as punishment and more efficient due to lower costs. The paper discusses the challenges of scaling cooperation to large groups and complex real-world dilemmas, emphasizing the importance of observability, local interactions, and psychological proximity. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This review is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment, reputation, and observability can promote cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but also cautions that gossip and reputation mechanisms may be equally or more efficient due to lower costs. The review does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects, so it should be used for contextual and mechanistic understanding rather than for parameterizing predictive models of efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of observability, reputation, and punishment as important moderators, but does not allow for direct estimation of their effects on efficiency.

- source: Janssen, M. A., DeCaro, D., & Lee, A. (2022). An Agent-Based Model of the Interaction between Inequality, Trust, and Communication in Common Pool Experiments. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial common-pool resource game with four players and no punishment or sanctioning, communication (chat) between rounds leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. Trust is modeled as a key mediator: communication increases trust, which reduces overharvesting and increases group earnings. The agent-based model, calibrated to experimental data, shows that a population with a majority of conditional cooperators (who respond to trust and inequality) best explains observed group outcomes. The model and data both show that communication leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use, even after communication ceases, due to persistent increases in trust. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, enabling communication (chat) in a common-pool resource game with four players and nine rounds leads to a large increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of chat/communication on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The model and empirical results suggest that communication alone can drive high efficiency via trust, but do not inform the marginal effect of adding punishment to such a game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for the effect of communication and trust in similar environments.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Chen, W. M. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study models a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, where individuals can be cooperators (who pay a cost to improve local environmental quality) or defectors (who do not pay and degrade the environment). The model explores how the ability of cooperators to repair the environment (delta), the destructiveness of defectors (gamma), and the cost of cooperation (c) affect the prevalence of cooperation. Key findings include: (1) Cooperation can only dominate if cooperator repair ability slightly exceeds defector destructiveness; (2) Increasing cooperator repair ability beyond a threshold can paradoxically reduce cooperation due to defector invasions; (3) Both very low and very high defector destructiveness can undermine cooperation; (4) Increasing cooperation cost generally reduces cooperation, but at low defector destructiveness, higher cost can sometimes help cooperation by reducing isolated cooperators and making clusters more robust. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed theoretical insights into how environmental feedback, spatial structure, and cost parameters affect the evolution of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual understanding of how non-punishment mechanisms and environmental feedbacks shape cooperation dynamics in spatial PGG variants.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., Chen, W. M., & Gu, C. L. (2022). Competition of punishment and reward among inequity-averse individuals in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that reputation-based indirect reciprocity can promote the spread of donation and cooperation behaviors in a donation game variant of the PGG, but this effect is limited by the costs of donation and cooperation, and by the presence of 'hypocrite' and 'realist' strategies. When only extreme altruists and egoists are present, cooperation rarely persists. The introduction of shunners (who conditionally cooperate based on reputation) allows for the spread of donation and cooperation under certain cost regimes. However, the addition of hypocrites (who donate for reputation but defect in daily interactions) and realists (who do not donate but cooperate in daily interactions) undermines the prevalence of donation, leading to more complex phase diagrams and sometimes the collapse of donation behavior. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of different strategies and the qualitative structure of phase transitions; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it explicitly excludes punishment mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of indirect reciprocity (reputation) as an alternative to punishment in promoting cooperation, and about how the presence of certain strategy types and cost parameters can affect the prevalence of prosocial behaviors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., reputation) can support cooperation under some conditions, but also that the presence of exploitative or non-donating cooperators can undermine these effects. The findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.
