- source: Sotnik, G., Choporov, S., & Shannon, T. (2023). The Role of Social Identity in a Population's Adoption of Prosocial Common-Pool Behavior. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that social identity, especially when combined with high group commitment, expands the parameter space (pressure-synergy combinations) where prosocial common-pool behavior is fully adopted. At low population density, both high- and low-commitment social identity models increase adoption and speed to full adoption compared to the baseline. At high density, only high-commitment agents show a strong positive effect; low-commitment agents may even reduce adoption. The model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral adoption rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how social identity and group commitment might affect the adoption of prosocial behavior in PGG-like environments, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Han, T. A. (2022). Institutional incentives for the evolution of committed cooperation: ensuring participation is as important as enhancing compliance. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (punishment or reward) can promote the evolution of cooperation and commitment compliance in a social dilemma with a prior commitment stage. Reward is generally more effective than punishment at promoting cooperation, especially when the cost of commitment is high or the benefit-to-cost ratio is large. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation in the commitment (not just compliance) further increases cooperation, particularly when participation is costly. The presence of noise in participation decisions can also stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are based on explicit payoff calculations and evolutionary stability analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in social dilemma games with a prior commitment stage, enabling punishment (or reward) increases group efficiency (cooperation frequency) compared to no incentives, provided the incentive budget is sufficient. Reward is more effective than punishment, especially as the cost of participation increases. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation (not just compliance) further increases efficiency, especially when participation is costly. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with a commitment or opt-in stage, and the model's explicit payoff calculations can be mapped to efficiency outcomes. However, the model is based on a two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a commitment stage, not a standard repeated public goods game, so predictions should be transferred with caution to multi-player, repeated settings.

- source: Jia, S. H., & Wang, Z. Y. (2025). An evolutionary game model with reputation threshold and reputation score to promote trust in the sharing economy. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that implementing a dynamic, reputation-based reward and punishment mechanism in a trust game on a square lattice network increases the proportion of trustworthy participants and can increase total group revenue (efficiency) compared to a baseline without such mechanisms. The effect depends on the reputation threshold: moderate thresholds maximize both trust and total revenue, while very high thresholds reduce revenue due to excessive punishment. The model demonstrates that, for a wide range of initial conditions, appropriate punishment/reward settings can drive the system to full trust and higher efficiency. The results are robust to population size. The model is not a standard PGG but is adjacent, and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in networked trust games with dynamic reputation-based punishment and reward, enabling punishment (and reward) can increase group efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, provided the punishment/reward parameters are set appropriately. The effect is non-monotonic: too much punishment (high threshold) reduces efficiency, while moderate levels maximize it. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and calibration of punishment/reward mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency gains from sanctions. The model is adjacent to PGGs but the efficiency outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2022). Costly incentives design from an institutional perspective: cooperation, sustainability and affluence. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with institutional (third-party) incentives, pure punishment is always sustainable and, when sufficiently strong, increases both the market size and the accumulated wealth of participants and the third party. Light punishment can lead to market shrinkage or collapse. Mixed incentives (reward plus punishment) can maximize the sum of accumulated wealth for both parties at moderate strength, but their sustainability depends on both incentive strength and agent rationality. Pure reward increases participant wealth but is unsustainable for the third party in this model. The results are robust across different levels of agent rationality, though higher rationality makes it easier to achieve high cooperation and affluence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated pairwise social dilemmas with institutional punishment, increasing the strength of punishment (i.e., higher fines for defection) leads to higher efficiency (measured as accumulated wealth and market size), provided the punishment is not too light. Light punishment can reduce efficiency by shrinking the market. Mixed incentives can further optimize total efficiency at moderate strength. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar institutional settings, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to defection. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, so results should be transferred with some caution.

- source: Han, T. A., Duong, M. H., & Perc, M. (2024). Evolutionary mechanisms that promote cooperation may not promote social welfare. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with peer or institutional incentives, punishment mechanisms (both peer and institutional) are often detrimental to social welfare, even when they increase cooperation. Peer punishment increases cooperation more than peer reward, but peer reward leads to higher social welfare. For institutional incentives, reward achieves positive social welfare across a broader range of parameters than punishment, even when both achieve similar cooperation levels. There is often an optimal, moderate level of incentive impact for maximizing social welfare, rather than maximizing cooperation or punishment strength. These findings are robust across mutation rates, selection intensities, and game configurations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments (here, the Prisoner's Dilemma) is likely to reduce efficiency (social welfare) compared to reward mechanisms, even if punishment increases cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of model parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially with high impact-to-cost ratio) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency relative to control, unless the punishment is extremely cost-effective and moderate in impact. The findings suggest that maximizing cooperation via punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and that reward mechanisms are generally preferable for social welfare. The results are based on theoretical models, not empirical data, but are directly relevant to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Mohlin, E., Rigos, A., & Weidenholzer, S. (2023). Emergence of specialized third-party enforcement. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that specialized third-party punishment can enable and sustain high-efficiency cooperative equilibria in social dilemmas where direct and indirect reciprocity fail. When enforcers are incentivized (via reputation and meta-enforcement) to punish defectors, producers cooperate, and both groups achieve higher payoffs. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the cost of information, and the destructiveness of enforcer conflict. If punishment is too costly or weak, or if enforcer conflict is not destructive enough, the system collapses to a low-efficiency defection equilibrium. The model also shows that the cooperative equilibrium is robust to the presence of alternative (non-cooperation-enforcing) reputation systems, but only if the key parameter thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment by specialized third-party enforcers can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but only if the punishment is sufficiently severe, not too costly, and enforcers are themselves disciplined (e.g., via meta-enforcement and reputation). The model clarifies which game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, information cost, severity of punishment, and the structure of enforcement interactions) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, provided the relevant costs and incentives are favorable. However, if punishment is weak, costly, or undisciplined, efficiency gains may not materialize.

- source: Yaman, A., Leibo, J. Z., Iacca, G., & Wan Lee, S. (2023). The emergence of division of labour through decentralized social sanctioning. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that decentralized social sanctioning, implemented as a matrix of role-based rules for reward and punishment, enables groups of self-interested agents to learn and maintain efficient divisions of labour. This leads to substantially higher average group payoff compared to groups without sanctioning, or with only altruistic or selfish learning. The effect is robust across different environmental conditions and is close to the theoretical upper bound. Complexity regularization can promote the evolution of simpler, convergent social norms without sacrificing group payoff. The model shows that sanctioning transforms the incentive structure, making it individually rational to take on critical but otherwise unrewarding roles, thus solving the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling decentralized social sanctioning (punishment and/or reward) in public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when critical roles are otherwise unrewarding. The effect is robust across a range of environmental parameters and is close to the optimal achievable by global optimization. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally adjacent, with payoff interdependence and social dilemmas. The findings support the prediction that, all else equal, enabling punishment (and/or reward) will increase efficiency relative to a control with sanctioning disabled, especially in settings where division of labour is required. The model also highlights the importance of norm complexity and the potential for simple sanctioning rules to be highly effective.

- source: Gioffré, A., & Tampieri, A. (2025). Community enforcement and the cost of cooperation. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement via grim punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with random matching, provided the cost of cooperation is not too low or too high. Specifically, if the cost-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold (less than 0.5), punishment is always credible and cooperation is an equilibrium for any population size and discount factor. For any environment, there exists a 'target cost of cooperation' that enables coordination on cooperation and makes punishment effective. When the cost of cooperation is endogenous, players can use it as a coordination device to achieve efficient outcomes. The results are entirely theoretical and provide explicit payoff-based conditions for when punishment increases group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with random matching and private monitoring, the introduction of credible punishment (grim trigger) can increase group efficiency to the cooperative level, provided the cost of cooperation is within a specific range. The key moderators are the cost-to-benefit ratio, population size, and discount factor. The results suggest that, for a wide range of parameter values, enabling punishment will move efficiency from the defection baseline to the cooperative optimum. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the cost of cooperation (analogous to MPCR in PGGs), population size, and the presence of punishment. However, the model is not a literal public goods game but an adjacent repeated PD, so some caution is warranted in direct quantitative transfer.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Melkonyan, T., Zeitoun, H., & Chater, N. (2022). The cognitive foundations of tacit commitments: A virtual bargaining model of dynamic interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dynamic games without external enforcement, the possibility of tacit commitment to punishment (as formalized by the virtual bargaining equilibrium) enables self-interested agents to achieve efficient, mutually beneficial outcomes that are not predicted by standard subgame perfect equilibrium. In games like the finitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, Centipede, and repeated Bertrand competition, VBE supports cooperation, collusion, or turn-taking, enforced by credible threats of punishment (even if costly). This leads to higher group payoffs and efficiency compared to the standard prediction. However, if players cannot commit to future punishment (SVBE), the threat is not credible and efficiency collapses to the Nash/subgame perfect level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling credible punishment (even if costly to the punisher) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated or sequential social dilemma games, relative to control conditions where punishment is not possible or not credible. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, but the logic applies to adjacent environments (e.g., repeated PD, Centipede, repeated Bertrand) that share key features with public goods games. The findings suggest that, when game design allows for credible commitment to punishment, efficiency is likely to be much higher than in control games without such commitment. The effect is robust to the absence of external enforcement or non-standard preferences. However, the model assumes agents reason as virtual bargainers; if they do not, or if commitment is not possible, efficiency gains disappear. The paper is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be credibly pre-committed.

- source: Han, T. A., Lenaerts, T. C., Santos, F. C., & Pereira, L. M. (2022). Voluntary safety commitments provide an escape from over-regulation in AI development. *Technology in Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated two-player (and generalizable to N-player) innovation race game, unconditional punishment of risk-taking (unsafe) behavior can reduce social welfare by over-regulating and suppressing beneficial innovation when risk is low. Introducing voluntary, binding commitments to safe behavior—where only those who commit and then defect are punished—enables the system to achieve high efficiency: safety compliance prevails when it is collectively optimal, and innovation is not stifled when risk-taking is preferred. Both peer and institutional punishment mechanisms are effective if they are targeted only at commitment violators. The model shows that the efficiency of punishment (cost to punisher vs. impact on punished) modulates these effects, but the voluntary commitment mechanism robustly improves outcomes across parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms—specifically, whether punishment is unconditional or conditional on voluntary commitment—has a major impact on group efficiency in public-goods-like innovation dilemmas. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary, binding commitments with targeted punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to unconditional punishment, especially in environments where the collective optimum varies with risk. The model also highlights the importance of punishment cost and effectiveness, and shows that allowing players to opt out of commitment (and thus avoid punishment) can prevent over-regulation and welfare loss. These insights are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in games with similar structure, even if the setting is not a classic PGG.

- source: Libois, F. (2022). Success and failure of communities managing natural resources: Static and dynamic inefficiencies. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that introducing sanctions (punishment) can increase efficiency in CPR games by enabling the enforcement of rules that prevent over-extraction (static inefficiency) and/or promote conservation (dynamic inefficiency). The effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the strength of the sanctioning mechanism relative to the incentives to deviate from cooperation. When sanctions are strong enough, the first-best (fully efficient) outcome can be achieved. With limited sanctions, only partial improvements are possible, and sometimes increasing the resource's value or growth rate can make enforcement harder, reducing efficiency. The model also shows that the number of players, extraction cost, and inequality among players all moderate the effect of sanctions on efficiency. In particular, more players or greater inequality can make conservation harder to enforce, while higher extraction costs can make it easier. The model provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in CPR-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment depends on the interplay between the sanctioning technology (maximum enforceable sanction), the number of players, the resource's growth rate, extraction cost, and initial value. If sanctions are strong enough relative to the incentives to deviate, enabling rules can be enforced and efficiency increases. If not, efficiency may not improve, or may even decrease if resource value/growth increases incentives to defect. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the logic of how punishment affects efficiency is highly transferable. The paper is especially informative for settings where the punishment mechanism is exogenous and bounded, and where resource dynamics (growth, conservation) are important. For prediction, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on whether the sanctioning parameters cross key thresholds relative to the game's other parameters.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2025). Cooperation in temporary partnerships. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random matching and temporary partnerships, combining direct punishment (within partnerships) and cyclical community punishment (across partnerships) can support full cooperation (efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, but only for sufficiently patient players (high discount factor). The milder cyclical punishment is less effective at deterring deviations in equilibrium (requires higher patience) but more effective at incentivizing punishment off-equilibrium (punishment is less costly, so more credible). In contrast, the classic grim punishment can sustain cooperation for less patient players when the temptation payoff is low, but fails when temptation is high. The length of partnerships (number of repeated rounds with the same partner) affects these thresholds: longer partnerships make cooperation harder to sustain for impatient players but make punishment more credible for patient players. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how the structure of repeated interactions (number of rounds per partnership, random rematching frequency), the type of punishment available (direct, community, grim), and player patience (discount factor) interact to determine whether punishment can sustain efficient (fully cooperative) outcomes. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling direct punishment (via temporary partnerships) can increase the set of conditions under which efficiency is sustainable, but only if players are sufficiently patient. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency depends on the interplay between the temptation payoff, the cost/structure of punishment, and the frequency of rematching. The results are most directly applicable to adjacent settings (repeated PD with random matching), but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or lab data, but its explicit equilibrium conditions and comparative statics are informative for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Lim, I. S., & Capraro, V. (2022). A Synergy of Institutional Incentives and Networked Structures in Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems. *Ieee Transactions on Circuits and Systems Ii-express Briefs*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment and network structure can interact synergistically to promote prosocial behavior and increase group efficiency (mean payoff) in a trust game. Specifically, the combination of even modest punishment and network structure can achieve full cooperation and maximal efficiency in parameter regions where neither mechanism alone would suffice. The required punishment level to achieve full cooperation is lower in structured populations than in well-mixed populations. The mean payoff is maximized at the threshold punishment level that induces full cooperation; increasing punishment beyond this point does not further increase efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of maintaining the incentive system. The paper provides explicit analytical boundaries for these effects as a function of the trust return (r), network degree (k), and punishment size (p).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a networked trust game (and by extension, in adjacent public-goods-like games) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is not too costly and the network structure is not too sparse. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is greater in structured populations than in well-mixed ones, and that the combination of network structure and punishment can achieve high efficiency at lower punishment levels than punishment alone. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the network structure (degree), the effectiveness and cost of punishment, and the underlying return to cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games but are likely to transfer to public goods games with similar strategic structure.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional division of labour on a tree network. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a division-of-labour game on a tree network, without sanctions, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (premierD), resulting in low efficiency. Introducing a defector sanction system (where defectors are punished with probability and severity) can make full cooperation or mixed equilibria stable, depending on the punishment parameters and cost/loss structure. As the effective punishment (probability times fine) increases, the system transitions from defection to mixed states to full cooperation, thus increasing efficiency. The premier sanction system (punishing the root node for any defection) is less effective at promoting cooperation. The model provides explicit stability conditions for each equilibrium, showing how efficiency depends on the sanction system and network structure. Adding more levels to the network (making it deeper) hinders cooperation and efficiency, while adding more parallel branches has an indeterminate effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like division-of-labour games on tree networks, enabling a defector-targeted punishment system increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong (high probability and/or fine). The effect is mediated by the cost of cooperation, the loss from defection, and the network structure. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (no punishment), introducing punishment will increase efficiency if the punishment is strong enough, but the effect may be partial (mixed equilibria) if punishment is moderate. The model also predicts that deeper (more levels) networks make it harder for punishment to achieve full cooperation. These insights can inform predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with hierarchical or supply-chain-like structure.

- source: Liu, S. Y., Zhang, C. Y., Li, K., & Zhang, J. L. (2022). Exploring the inducement for social dilemma and cooperation promotion mechanisms in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in structured populations playing iterated prisoner's dilemma with memory-one strategies, increased randomness and heterogeneity in network topology (e.g., moving from regular lattice to scale-free networks) lead to a higher prevalence of defectors and lower collective average payoff (social welfare). The key behavioral parameter is p4, the probability of cooperating after mutual defection; low p4 is associated with 'dangerous' strategies that dominate in more random/heterogeneous networks and drive payoffs toward the minimum. The authors introduce two mechanisms: (1) enforcement, which forbids p4 < 0.3, and (2) punishment, which imposes a fine on agents with p4 < 0.3. Both mechanisms, especially with sufficient punishment intensity, eliminate dangerous strategies and raise the collective average payoff substantially, regardless of network structure. The effect is robust across random regular, ER random, and BA scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: Although the paper studies the iterated prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the structure is closely related, and the main outcome—collective average payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible—is directly analogous to efficiency. The findings provide strong evidence that introducing punishment (with sufficient intensity) in a repeated social dilemma robustly increases group efficiency, especially in environments where network structure would otherwise favor defection. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment targets low willingness to cooperate after mutual defection. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its strength: weak punishment may not eliminate low-cooperation strategies, but moderate to strong punishment does. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to network-induced defection.

- source: Uchida, S., Sasaki, T., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2024). Evolution of Pay-It-Forward in the Presence of the Temptation to Free-Ride. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a donation game with institutional punishment, the evolution of cooperation and pay-it-forward strategies is strongly affected by the cognitive model of the players. Under EUT, only strong punishment (high fine or high probability) can sustain cooperation, and pay-it-forward strategies are not stable. Under PT, even weak or infrequent punishment is sufficient to deter free-riding, because players overweight small probabilities of being punished. This leads to the emergence and stability of pay-it-forward strategies and higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population. The model shows bi-stability, with both cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria possible depending on parameters and initial conditions, but overall, punishment increases efficiency more under PT than under EUT.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games (here, a donation game) will increase group efficiency, especially when players are subject to prospect-theoretic biases. The effect is more pronounced when punishment is mild or infrequent, as real human players may overweight the risk of being punished. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the payoff-based outcomes are directly analyzed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (even with low probability or low severity) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly if the control game has low efficiency due to free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of psychological factors (probability weighting) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional linear division of labour of finite roles. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a linear division-of-labor game, the introduction of a defector sanction system (punishing defectors with a fine, with some probability of detection) can stabilize the all-cooperation equilibrium and thus maximize group efficiency, provided the expected punishment is sufficiently high relative to the cost of cooperation. The first role sanction system (punishing only the first role regardless of who defects) is less effective and only promotes cooperation when the cost of cooperation decreases downstream. The benefit from cooperation does not affect the stability of cooperation or efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which each equilibrium is stable, showing that punishment can transform a system from one where defection is stable (and efficiency is low) to one where cooperation and high efficiency are stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical support for predicting that, in public-goods-like environments with sequential or asymmetric roles, introducing a punishment mechanism that targets defectors (with sufficient expected punishment) will increase group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of roles (player count) and the cost structure, provided the expected punishment exceeds the maximum cooperation cost. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment system (who is punished, detection probability, and fine size) is critical for achieving efficiency gains. The results are most directly applicable to settings with sequential dependencies and asymmetric roles, but the logic may transfer to some PGG variants. The model does not address repeated finite rounds, communication, or stochastic group sizes, so predictions should be cautious if those features are present.

- source: Wang, S. X., Cao, M., & Chen, X. J. (2025). Optimally Combined Incentive for Cooperation Among Interacting Agents in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal protocol for combining reward and punishment to promote cooperation is time-invariant and depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the initial and target cooperation levels. When the initial cooperation level is high, pure punishment is most cost-effective; when it is low, pure reward is more efficient; and for intermediate cases, a specific mix of reward and punishment minimizes the total cost. The minimal cost protocol is analytically derived for both well-mixed (complete graph) and regular network populations. The results are robust across different network structures. The findings are directly relevant to predicting the efficiency of institutional punishment and/or reward interventions in repeated social dilemma settings, though the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of punishment (and reward) interventions in repeated social dilemma games, especially when the outcome of interest is the cost required to achieve a target level of cooperation. While the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game, the structure and logic of institutional incentives are closely related. The results suggest that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the initial cooperation level, the cost-to-benefit ratio, and the network structure. The paper does not report group payoff or surplus in the standard PGG sense, but its focus on cumulative cost as a measure of efficiency is highly relevant for predicting the cost-effectiveness of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., & Bi, Y. (2024). The influence of the heterogeneities of social institutions and individuals' tendency to establish social institutions on cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous social institutions (pool punishment, anti-social punishment, and social security/reward) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma framework can promote the evolution of pro-social groups and suppress second-order free-riding. The model shows that increasing the cost of institutional investment and the growth factor of the institutional pool generally promotes cooperation and higher average payoffs, but only up to an optimal point. The introduction of a non-wasteful model with an upper limit on individual punishment/reward further improves the efficiency of the system by reducing resource waste and allowing pro-social groups to survive under harsher social dilemmas. The network (spatial) structure enhances these effects compared to well-mixed populations. While the main outcomes are strategy proportions, the paper also analyzes average payoffs, showing that institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can increase group welfare compared to baseline scenarios without such institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment and reward institutions—including their cost, effectiveness, and resource allocation rules—can significantly affect the efficiency (average payoff) of groups in social dilemma games. While the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanisms are closely related, especially for pool punishment and institutional reward. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (and reward) institutions, especially with non-wasteful resource allocation and optimal parameter settings, will generally increase group efficiency compared to control conditions without such institutions. The results also highlight the importance of network structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the presence of anti-social punishment as moderators. However, the lack of direct efficiency ratios and the use of a prisoner's dilemma rather than a PGG limit the direct quantitative transferability to PGG-like environments.

- source: Rubin, H. (2022). When it pays to punish in the evolution of honesty and cooperation. *Synthese*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses evolutionary game theory and simulations to analyze how different forms of costly punishment (punishing lying vs. punishing defection) affect the evolution of honesty and cooperation in a repeated signaling/donation game. It finds that: (1) Punishment of lying increases honesty but often reduces the prevalence of cooperation, because the costs of punishing and being punished can outweigh the benefits of increased honesty. (2) Punishment of defection (as in standard strong reciprocity models) increases cooperation and group success, consistent with prior findings in stag hunt and repeated PD games. (3) The effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on game parameters (number of rounds, benefit of donation, lie detection probability, punishment cost and effectiveness). (4) When both types of punishment are available, the results are intermediate. The paper highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and can be negative when punishment targets lying rather than defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important cautionary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with multi-stage cooperation (e.g., signaling plus donation). If punishment targets lying (dishonest signaling), it may increase honesty but can reduce overall cooperation and thus efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and lie detection is imperfect. If punishment targets defection (failure to cooperate), it is more likely to increase cooperation and efficiency, as in standard public goods or stag hunt models. For prediction tasks, the design dimension 'punishment target' (lying vs. defection) is critical. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the number of rounds, and the benefit of cooperation all moderate the effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), especially when punishment targets lying.

- source: Ntemos, K., Pikramenos, G., & Kalouptsidis, N. (2022). Dynamic Information Sharing and Punishment Strategies. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic information sharing games with two agents, cooperation (information sharing) is not sustainable in finite-horizon settings, but can be sustained in infinite-horizon settings using punishment strategies (CGT). The equilibrium region where cooperation is optimal is characterized, and its size increases as the cost of sharing decreases or the discount factor increases. Theoretical results show that the maximal equilibrium region is unique and can be computed iteratively. Simulations confirm that lower costs and more informative observations lead to larger cooperation regions. Thus, enabling punishment strategies increases the efficiency of the system (total expected information gain minus cost) compared to the baseline where no sharing occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment strategies in repeated/dynamic information sharing games can increase efficiency (total payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, provided the game is sufficiently long (infinite horizon) and the cost of sharing is not too high. The effect is more positive when the cost of sharing is low and the discount factor is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games: the 'public good' is information, and the payoff is information gain minus cost. The results suggest that, in similar dynamic games, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially as the game becomes more repeated and the cost of cooperation decreases. However, the model is limited to two players, binary actions, and does not include monetary payoffs or group sizes greater than two.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2023). Optimization of institutional incentives for cooperation in structured populations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward can be tuned to achieve near-full cooperation in structured populations, with the optimal protocol being time-invariant and identical for both types of incentives under a given update rule. The minimal cumulative cost to the institution depends on the initial fraction of cooperators: punishment is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is high, while reward is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is low. The results are supported by analytical derivations and simulations across regular, random, small-world, and scale-free networks. The main outcome is the cumulative cost to the institution, which is a proxy for efficiency from the perspective of implementing cooperation-promoting interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the cost-effectiveness of punishment (and reward) depends on game parameters such as network structure, update rule, and initial cooperation level. While it does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, its focus on the cumulative cost to achieve high cooperation is closely related and can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar structured games. The results suggest that, with optimally tuned punishment, high cooperation (and thus high group efficiency) can be achieved at lower institutional cost when initial cooperation is already substantial. The findings are most directly applicable to structured Prisoner's Dilemma settings with institutional (not peer) punishment or reward, and may be less directly transferable to standard public goods games.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hernandez, I., Cohen, D., Gruschow, K., Nowak, A., Gelfand, M. J., & Borkowski, W. (2022). The Importance of Being Unearnest: Opportunists and the Making of Culture. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that (1) more severe punishment for noncooperation reduces the amount of voluntary cooperation in a population, both in agent-based simulations and in a human experiment; (2) raising the standard for what counts as sufficient cooperation to avoid punishment also reduces voluntary cooperation; (3) the presence of opportunists is necessary for strong reciprocators to thrive, but when punishment is too severe, opportunists proliferate and strong reciprocators decline; (4) these dynamics help explain why tight or collectivist cultures with strong norms and sanctions may have lower social capital (voluntary cooperation) than expected. The main outcome is the amount of voluntary cooperation, not direct group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that harsher punishment can reduce overall welfare by crowding out voluntary, uncoerced cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with punishment, increasing the severity of punishment or raising the bar for what counts as sufficient cooperation can reduce the amount of voluntary cooperation, and thus likely reduce group efficiency or welfare. The presence of punishment is necessary for sustaining cooperation, but making punishment too severe or standards too high backfires. For prediction tasks, this suggests a non-monotonic or negative effect of increasing punishment severity on efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or standards are high. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible payoffs, so the mapping to efficiency is indirect. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment severity and standards on voluntary cooperation and, by extension, on group efficiency in similar multi-agent, multi-round games.

- source: Rumble, A. C., Willcox, K., Imada, H., & Yansen, D. (2022). Beyond Reciprocity: Forgiveness, Generosity, and Punishment in Continuing Dyadic Interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from simulation and behavioral studies of dyadic repeated games (mainly Prisoner's Dilemma and its continuous variants) to argue that punishment, while sometimes increasing cooperation, generally reduces average payoffs and is less effective than generous or forgiving strategies for maintaining cooperation and maximizing outcomes. Costly punishment is found to lower group welfare and does not restore cooperation as effectively as non-punitive responses. The review highlights that punishment also carries social and emotional costs, making it less desirable in ongoing dyadic relationships. Generosity and forgiveness, by contrast, are more likely to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, especially when noise or uncertainty is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that, in dyadic repeated games (especially noisy or continuous-choice environments), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency or group payoff compared to control conditions without punishment. The negative effect is attributed to both the direct material cost of punishment and the escalation of retaliation cycles, as well as social/emotional fallout. The review suggests that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially if costly and in dyads) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency, unless the punishment is very cheap or the environment is highly structured to avoid retaliation spirals. The findings are most directly relevant to dyadic (two-player) repeated games, and less so to larger public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed may generalize to small groups.

- source: Zu, J. J., Xu, F. X., Jin, T., & Xiang, W. (2022). Reward and Punishment Mechanism with weighting enhances cooperation in evolutionary games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of a reward and punishment mechanism with weighting (RPMW) increases the frequency of cooperation in evolutionary games (PDG, SDG, SHG), both in well-mixed and lattice populations. The effect is stronger with higher intensity (alpha) and a higher proportion of supervisors (beta). The combination of reward and punishment is more effective than either alone in promoting cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, and that the effect is stronger with higher punishment/reward intensity and more supervisors. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific game structure is not a public goods game but rather two-player matrix games (PDG, SDG, SHG), so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2025). Effects of an update mechanism based on combinatorial memory and high-reputation learning objects on the evolution of. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a social monitoring mechanism (SMM) that rewards judges based on the observed cooperation rate among participants leads to higher levels of cooperation and lower corruption in the simulated two-layer network. The effect is robust across different parameter settings, including reward/fine amounts, the social reward criterion, the presence of zealots, and the strength of the reward factor. SMM is more effective than simply increasing rewards/fines in the absence of SMM. The model is applied to both Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game variants, showing similar qualitative results. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation/corruption rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which reward third-party punishers based on group cooperation rates can increase cooperation and reduce corruption in environments structurally similar to the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be interpreted as informative about behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency effects. The model is not a standard public goods game, but the two-layer structure and the focus on punishment/reward mechanisms are adjacent to PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that adding a social monitoring/reward mechanism for punishers may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified.

- source: Wang, C. Y., & Cui, W. F. (2022). Supervision for the Public Health Services for Older Adults Under the Background of Government Purchasing: An Evolutionary Game Analysis Framework. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a principal-agent setting with information asymmetry, static punishment or reward mechanisms are insufficient to ensure full compliance (self-discipline) by service providers. Dynamic mechanisms, where the intensity of punishment or reward depends on the observed behavior, can stabilize higher levels of self-discipline. Specifically, higher punishment intensity increases the proportion of self-discipline, while higher reward intensity can decrease it due to moral hazard and adverse selection. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that dynamic sanctioning mechanisms are more effective at promoting compliance than static ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (or self-discipline) depends on the design of the sanctioning mechanism, particularly whether it is static or dynamic. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is structurally similar in that it models a social dilemma with potential for free-riding and the use of sanctions. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like environments, dynamic punishment mechanisms are likely to be more effective at increasing cooperation than static ones, and that increasing punishment intensity generally increases compliance. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of punishment design and intensity on compliance behavior.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Jiang, X. H., & Zheng, W. Q. (2024). Dynamic strategies for collaborative governance of rural environments: a simulation study on the most effective mechanisms. *Environment Development and Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that under a static reward and punishment mechanism (SRPM), the system does not reach a stable equilibrium; strategies of all parties fluctuate periodically. Under a dynamic reward and punishment mechanism (DRPM), the system converges to a stable mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium (MSNE), interpreted as an evolutionarily stable strategy (ESS). Increasing the upper limit of punishments for illegal emissions by village enterprises significantly increases the probability of legal emissions and reduces the need for strict government regulation and active supervision by villagers. Increasing the upper limit of rewards for villagers' active supervision increases their supervision probability but may reduce legal compliance by enterprises and government regulatory effort. The DRPM is recommended for more effective and stable governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that dynamic, behavior-dependent punishment mechanisms (analogous to more flexible or responsive punishment rules in public goods games) can stabilize cooperative behavior (legal emissions) and reduce the need for costly monitoring and enforcement. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The main outcomes are strategy probabilities, not payoffs. The findings suggest that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech) and using dynamic punishment rules may improve stability and compliance, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The results are most relevant for understanding how punishment design (cost, tech, dynamic vs static) may affect behavioral outcomes in multi-agent governance games.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher.
