The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Goodman, J. R. (2023). The problem of opportunity. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (strong reciprocity), cooperation can be maintained at high levels when only overt defectors are present. However, when agents can mimic cooperators or defect covertly (evading detection and punishment), the population can be dominated by individuals who are not true cooperators, even though the observed rate of cooperation remains high. The models highlight that the opportunity to defect undetected undermines the effectiveness of punishment-based strategies in ensuring genuine prosociality. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but raises concerns that high observed cooperation rates in experimental games may not indicate high group welfare or internalized prosocial preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution for interpreting high cooperation rates in punishment-enabled games as evidence of increased efficiency or group welfare. It shows that punishment can maintain high cooperation in simple models, but that the introduction of undetectable defection strategies (mimicry, covert defection) can allow free-riding to persist without reducing the observed cooperation rate. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment may not always translate to higher efficiency, especially if the game design allows for undetected defection. However, the models are dyadic PDs, not public goods games, and do not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper.

- source: Shen, C., He, Z. X., Shi, L., Wang, Z., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Prosocial punishment bots breed social punishment in human players. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a fraction of bots that always cooperate and punish defectors (CP) into a population of normal players in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma can significantly increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment among normal players. This effect is stronger when bots are placed on high-degree nodes in scale-free networks or when players have a conformist bias (copying the majority). The effect is robust to different network structures, learning rules, and parameter values. However, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report the effect of punishment on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment (specifically, prosocial punishment implemented by bots) can increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment and cooperation in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding how network structure, imitation rules, and the presence of committed punishers affect the spread of prosocial punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhao, X., & Zou, H. (2025). Research on the Evolutionary Game of Multi-Body Co-Innovation in Green Innovation Ecosystems. *Polish Journal of Environmental Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that government-imposed rewards (subsidies) and punishments (fines) both promote the stability of collaborative innovation among the three actors. There is an optimal benefit-sharing ratio for collaboration, and the effect of government intervention is strongest when both rewards and punishments are used together. The model shows threshold effects for punishment severity, and that the stability of cooperation is sensitive to the distribution of benefits and the magnitude of policy interventions. However, the main outcomes are the evolutionary stability of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task. It shows that introducing punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases the stability and likelihood of cooperation in a multi-actor game with public-goods-like features. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The results suggest that, in similar multi-actor public-goods-like environments, punishment (especially when combined with reward) is likely to increase cooperation and system stability, which may be associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Gao, D. D., & Guo, W. (2025). Evolutionary game and simulation analysis of tripartite subjects in public health emergencies under government reward and punishment mechanisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that government reward and punishment mechanisms (subsidies and fines) for pharmaceutical companies and the public promote compliant production and cooperation in prevention and control, respectively. Increasing the magnitude of rewards or punishments accelerates the convergence to full compliance and cooperation. The initial willingness of parties to participate also affects the speed of convergence. However, the model does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are in terms of the probability of adopting certain strategies and the speed of convergence to stable equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that increasing the strength of punishment and reward mechanisms can promote compliance and cooperation in a multi-party public-goods-like setting. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings may inform the qualitative direction of punishment effects in similar environments, but cannot be used for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., Wang, Z., & Wang, R. W. (2022). Asymmetric strategy setup solve the Prisoner's Dilemma of the evolution of mutualism. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing asymmetric carrot-stick strategies (where one population can reward or punish the other) leads to a rich set of evolutionary outcomes, including stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, bistability, and dominance, depending on parameter values. The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. The model suggests that asymmetric sanctions can help maintain cooperation and avoid the tragedy of the commons, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and focuses on strategy frequencies and stability, not on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the availability and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms (especially when asymmetric) can qualitatively change the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment (and reward) can stabilize cooperation under certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the presence and magnitude of punishment and reward (relative to benefit and cost) are important moderators, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment/reward can sustain cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, J. Y., Wu, W. H., Li, Z. Z., Wang, W. X., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Data-driven evolutionary game models for the spread of fairness and cooperation in heterogeneous networks. *Frontiers in Psychiatry*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment, when amplified by network heterogeneity and increased inequality aversion (e.g., via oxytocin), can spread through a network and promote higher levels of fairness and cooperation. This effect is observed in both the ultimatum game and the two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment, but not in the trust game, where the absence of punishment means that increased trust does not diffuse. The models fit experimental data well for behavioral outcomes (offers, cooperation rates, punishment rates), but do not directly analyze or report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward/trust mechanisms for promoting prosocial behavior in networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in networked social dilemmas (such as public goods or prisoner's dilemma variants) increases cooperation and fairness, especially when network heterogeneity allows central nodes to amplify the effect. However, because the paper is theoretical and focuses on behavioral outcomes (not efficiency or group payoff), it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment is more effective than reward in promoting prosocial outcomes, but efficiency implications must be inferred from increased cooperation and fairness, not directly measured. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., network structure, inequality aversion), but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Li, B. F., Zhang, X., Shi, Z. Y., & Wei, W. (2023). Justice strategy can promote cooperation with the joint of dynamic aspiration in social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a justice strategy (where players punish defectors at equal cost) alongside dynamic aspiration updating can suppress or eliminate defectors and promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness depends on the initial aspiration level: higher aspirations lead to fewer defectors and more justicers. The justice strategy can dominate even when initially rare, especially when defectors form clusters. The model is agent-based and simulation-driven, with outcomes reported as the stable fractions of each strategy. No aggregate payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (even when costly to the punisher) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, especially when combined with dynamic aspirations. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or payoff, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, though the mechanisms are related. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which punishment can affect cooperation, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, W., He, Z. X., Li, X. G., & Shi, L. (2023). Adaptive exit facilitates the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively exit the game based on their payoff relative to aspiration can significantly increase the prevalence of cooperation, even when punishment is weak. There is an optimal aspiration level at which adaptive exit best promotes cooperation. Adaptive exit protects cooperators from exploitation by defectors and reduces the burden of punishment on punishers by increasing defectors' exit probability. The model shows that adaptive exit can eliminate second-order free-riding and that the interplay between exit and punishment is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that adaptive exit mechanisms and the interaction between exit and punishment can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling exit (or similar mechanisms) may increase cooperation and reduce the cost of punishment, but do not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Zhao, C. Y., Zheng, G. Z., Zhang, C., Zhang, J. Q., & Chen, L. (2024). Emergence of cooperation under punishment: A reinforcement learning perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can promote cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning agents, but only if the punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly. If the cost of punishment is high or the punishment intensity is low, introducing punishment can actually reduce cooperation below the baseline with no punishment. The system can exhibit both continuous and discontinuous transitions in cooperation as punishment parameters are varied. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; group efficiency or total payoff is not reported. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, intensity, probability) affect cooperation rates in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning. While the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that punishment does not always improve group outcomes: if punishment is too costly or too weak, it can reduce cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic and sensitive to cost-effectiveness. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings should be used as qualitative, mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Sun, X. P., Han, L. F., Wang, M., Liu, S. X., & Shen, Y. (2023). Social exclusion with antisocial punishment in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment by investors can induce a stable coexistence of punishing investors and trustworthy trustees in a hierarchical N-player trust game. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting trust depends on both the intensity of punishment and the fraction of investors in the population. For low punishment intensity, an intermediate fraction of investors is optimal for promoting trust, while for high punishment intensity, a higher fraction of investors is more effective. Increasing punishment intensity generally enlarges the attraction domain of the coexistence state, but only up to a point. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but interprets the stable coexistence of trust and trustworthiness as a positive social outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote trust and trustworthiness in a hierarchical trust game, with the effect moderated by punishment intensity and the fraction of investors. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is indirect. The findings suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment, as well as the composition of the population, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperative behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can improve group outcomes under certain conditions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Nie, J. D., Wang, J., Niu, H. D., & Xia, C. Y. (2022). Impact of multi-step punishment on the spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that multi-step punishment (punishment lasting multiple time steps for low-reputation individuals) can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, but only if the punishment duration is neither too short nor too long. There is an optimal punishment time window that maximizes the frequency of cooperation. Too short punishment fails to deter defection, while too long punishment can backfire and reduce cooperation. The intensity of punishment (how much payoffs are reduced for punished individuals) is the dominant factor in promoting cooperation. The model does not include a cost for punishment, and does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. All results are based on the proportion of cooperators in the population.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates, especially with optimal duration and sufficient intensity, but the absence of punishment cost and lack of payoff analysis limit its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about punishment's effect on cooperation, but not on efficiency or welfare. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

  findings: The study finds that periodic update rules with Q-learning and a punishment mechanism can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Lower punishment costs and shorter update intervals are especially effective at promoting cooperation. The model is robust across different network structures. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff, and the results are based on agent-based simulations rather than empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms, when combined with learning and periodic update rules, can increase cooperation rates in spatial games. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that lower punishment costs and more frequent updating are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related models, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Guo, T., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2023). Self-organization in mobile populations promotes the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that migration in spatially structured populations promotes the evolution and prevalence of pro-social punishment (altruistic punishment) and cooperation, especially when individuals migrate to maximize potential gains rather than avoid losses. There is an optimal population density for this effect. Migration can sustain pro-social punishment even when punishment efficiency (fine/cost ratio) is low, as long as the absolute amount of punishment is high. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the prevalence of pro-social punishment and cooperation is interpreted as a positive outcome for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling migration or spatial self-organization can enhance the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, especially under certain migration preferences and population densities. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which migration and spatial structure interact with punishment, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guan, K. X., Chen, Y. Y., Zheng, W. J., Zeng, L. L., & Ye, H. (2022). Costly signals can facilitate cooperation and punishment in the prisoner?s dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with costly signaling, punishment as a costly signal can become prevalent if the signal is strong and noise is low, but this does not necessarily increase cooperation. Without punishment, cooperation can dominate if the cost-benefit ratio is favorable and noise is low. The introduction of punishment (when its cost is converted into a strong costly signal) increases the frequency of punishment and reduces defection, but does not increase cooperation. The model does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in two-player prisoner's dilemma games with costly signaling, but does not directly address efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can reduce defection and increase the prevalence of punishment strategies if the punishment signal is strong, but does not necessarily increase cooperation or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the relevance is limited and indirect, as the model is not a PGG and does not report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zhu, W. Q., Pan, Q. H., Song, S., & He, M. F. (2023). Effects of exposure-based reward and punishment on the evolution of cooperation in prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an infinite well-mixed population, exposure-based punishment (and especially the combination of reward and punishment) can promote the evolution of cooperation, but only if the cost of exposure is less than the punishment for defection. Rewarding exposed cooperators alone is insufficient to promote cooperation. There are threshold and nonlinear effects: simultaneous reward and punishment is most effective, and there are optimal values for exposure cost and reward/punishment strength. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation can be sustained, but all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment (and especially combined with reward) can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting with exposure-based mechanisms. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and focuses on cooperation rates, so its main value is as indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation under certain cost/benefit conditions. It does not provide quantitative predictions for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Zhang, Y. J., Zeng, Z. Y., Pi, B., & Feng, M. Y. (2023). An evolutionary game with revengers and sufferers on complex networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a revenge-based costly punishment mechanism in the PDG on networks increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the cost to the punisher is low and the cost to the punished is high. The effect is robust across network types (square lattice and small-world), cost-to-benefit ratios, and network sizes. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not report on group welfare, surplus, or total earnings, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, revenge-based costly punishment) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games on networks. However, because the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model structure and parameterization may inform mechanistic expectations, but quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Zhang, M., Zhang, X., Qu, C. Q., Wang, G. H., & Lu, X. (2022). The combination of social reward and punishment is conducive to the cooperation and heterogeneity of social relations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that a combined reward-punishment mechanism (RPM) in a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma can significantly increase the average frequency of cooperation compared to either pure punishment or pure reward alone. Pure punishment only promotes cooperation under high temptation to defect, while pure reward can actually undermine cooperation. The combination of both, with appropriately tuned intensities, sustains high cooperation even under strong temptation to defect. The model also shows that the RPM leads to spontaneous emergence of network heterogeneity, with cooperators becoming influential hubs. However, the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence and calibration of both punishment and reward mechanisms can strongly affect cooperation rates in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that in environments where both punishment and reward are available and appropriately balanced, cooperation (and likely efficiency) can be sustained at higher levels than with either mechanism alone, especially under high temptation to defect. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Chen, L., Sun, J. J., Li, K., & Li, Q. R. (2022). Research on the effectiveness of monitoring mechanism for yield to pedestrian based on system dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Without external control, the system exhibits persistent oscillations in the probability of drivers yielding to pedestrians, and TMD monitoring disappears over time. (2) Introducing dynamic punishment (fines that increase with non-compliance) suppresses oscillations and increases compliance, but does not fully eliminate violations. (3) Combining dynamic punishment and reward (penalty-incentive control) leads to full compliance (all drivers yield) and eliminates the need for monitoring or pedestrian supervision. (4) Alternatively, a sufficiently high static monitoring rate by TMD (above a threshold) can also achieve full compliance. The results are framed in terms of behavioral probabilities and system stability, not aggregate efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that dynamic punishment and/or reward mechanisms can stabilize and maximize compliance in a multi-agent system with monitoring and sanctioning, analogous to public goods games with punishment and reward. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative effect of punishment and reward on stabilizing cooperation and eliminating violations, especially in systems with endogenous monitoring and third-party enforcement. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the outcome mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Huo, H., & Liu, X. Y. (2024). Behavioral decision-making of government, agricultural product producers, and consumers on agricultural product quality and safety regulation in a digital environment. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that severe government penalties for non-compliance (punishment), sufficient incentives, and reduced costs for producers to build traceability systems are key to achieving stable cooperation among stakeholders. Depending on the relative size of these parameters, the system can stabilize in different equilibria: (Not regulate, Not build, Satisfied), (Regulate, Not build, Satisfied), or (Regulate, Build, Satisfied). The presence and strength of punishment (penalties) are critical in shifting the equilibrium toward higher compliance by producers. However, the outcomes are reported as stable strategy profiles, not as explicit efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and severity of punishment (penalties) can shift the equilibrium from non-compliance to compliance in a multi-actor regulatory game. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or explicit payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that increasing punishment can increase compliance, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff is not quantified. The model structure and parameter dependencies may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment in similar multi-actor regulatory environments.

- source: Wang, G. C., Su, Q., & Wang, L. (2022). Evolution of cooperation with joint liability. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that joint liability, when combined with costly punishment, strongly promotes the evolution of cooperation in a lending game. Punishment alone is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but joint liability provides incentives for individuals to punish defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and low levels of defection. The condition for cooperation to evolve is relaxed from β−γ>4c (without joint liability) to β>γ (with joint liability and low tolerability for defection). The effect is robust to model extensions, including intragroup donation games (Prisoner's Dilemma). The abundance of cooperators and punishers is maximized at intermediate values of selection intensity, population size, and mutation rate. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure and outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the combination of joint liability and costly punishment can greatly increase cooperation in group settings similar to, but not identical with, public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment is much more effective at promoting cooperation when joint liability is present, and that the threshold for punishment to be effective is lower. However, because the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of realized to maximum group payoff, the results should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The paper is most informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group-level liability interact, and for predicting that punishment will be more effective in environments with joint liability or similar group-level consequences.

- source: Wang, J., Liu, Z., Xu, Y., & Li, X. P. (2025). Dynamic evolution in multi-player networked trust games with graded punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a graded punishment mechanism in a networked trust game simulation can effectively reduce untrustworthy behavior and increase the density of investment and trust. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost to punishers and the severity of punishment. Graded punishment outperforms traditional punishment in eliminating untrustworthy agents and increasing simulated group trust and total wealth. However, if punishment costs are too high, investment and trust collapse. The results are sensitive to network topology, with regular lattices supporting higher total wealth than scale-free networks. All findings are based on agent-based simulations, not real-world or laboratory experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that graded punishment mechanisms can increase simulated group trust and total wealth in a networked trust game, especially when punishment is not too costly. However, because the study is simulation-based and uses a trust game variant (not a standard PGG), its findings should be interpreted as suggestive rather than directly predictive for real-world or experimental PGGs. The results highlight the importance of the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and suggest that graded (rather than uniform) punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation and group payoff in networked environments. The paper does not provide empirical data for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but its modeling results may inform hypotheses about the role of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network structure.

- source: Jia, C., Zhang, R. X., & Wang, D. (2023). Evolutionary game of cooperative behavior among social capitals in PPP projects: A complex network perspective. *Ain Shams Engineering Journal*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing the default penalty (punishment) leads to higher rates of cooperation among social capitals in a networked prisoner's dilemma setting. Higher network density reduces cooperation, and initial cooperation probability can promote cooperation only when the temptation to defect (betrayal income) is not too high. The results are based on simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not empirical data. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that cooperation is important for the efficiency of PPP projects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that introducing or increasing punishment in a networked prisoner's dilemma can increase cooperation rates, which the authors argue is beneficial for project efficiency. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report results in terms of efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanism (cooperation) rather than direct prediction of efficiency outcomes. The model's focus on network structure, punishment, and cooperation may inform qualitative expectations about the direction of efficiency effects when punishment is enabled, but does not provide direct predictive data for the downstream task.

- source: Wang, Q. E., Lai, W., Ding, M. M., & Qiu, Q. (2022). Research on Cooperative Behavior of Green Technology Innovation in Construction Enterprises Based on Evolutionary Game. *Buildings*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stronger punishment mechanisms (higher penalty and indirect punishment) increase the likelihood that both parties will adopt positive cooperation strategies, thus reducing opportunistic behavior. Similarly, higher willingness to cooperate, greater positive spillover (sharing), and more favorable income distribution also promote positive cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of the probability or stability of cooperation strategies, not in terms of group efficiency or total payoff. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves cooperation with spillovers and punishment, but is tailored to the context of construction enterprise innovation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that increasing punishment intensity (both direct and indirect) makes positive cooperation more likely in a repeated game with spillovers, which is structurally similar to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so its value is in supporting the mechanism that punishment can deter defection and promote cooperation under certain parameter regimes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment, sharing, and income distribution interact to affect cooperation, but less directly useful for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, Q. W. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback in regular networked Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback can lead to three types of system states: (1) stable full defection with low probability of effective punishment, (2) stable full cooperation with high probability of effective punishment, and (3) bi-stability where both outcomes are possible depending on initial conditions. Increasing the speed of environmental feedback (i.e., how quickly the probability of effective punishment updates in response to behavior) can shift the system from defection to cooperation in the bi-stable regime. The model highlights the importance of punishment effectiveness, punishment cost, network degree (number of neighbors), and feedback speed in shaping cooperation dynamics. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback affect cooperation in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that punishment mechanisms with high effectiveness and fast feedback can promote cooperation, especially in structured populations. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings may inform the expected direction of efficiency changes (via increased cooperation), but quantitative predictions for efficiency require caution.

- source: Wang, Y. F., & Meng, X. Z. (2023). Evolutionary game dynamics of cooperation in prisoner?s dilemma with time delay. *Mathematical Biosciences and Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a 2-player prisoner's dilemma, adding a penalty for mutual defection (punishment) leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors when the penalty exceeds the cost of cooperation (a > c). The equilibrium proportion of cooperators increases with the penalty size. When time delays are introduced, the system can undergo a Hopf bifurcation: for small delays, the equilibrium is stable, but for delays above a critical threshold (which decreases as penalty increases), the system oscillates between cooperation and defection. Adding mutation (random switching between strategies) slightly reduces the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, but does not fundamentally change the qualitative dynamics. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency of cooperation and the stability of these frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that increasing the severity of punishment for defection in a prisoner's dilemma increases the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, which is typically associated with higher group payoff in such games. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence. The results suggest that, in similar 2-player games, enabling punishment (with sufficient severity) will increase cooperation rates, and thus likely improve efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. However, if time delays are present and large, the system may become unstable and oscillate, which could reduce average efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with 2 players, no communication, continuous contribution/strategy choice, and deterministic evolutionary dynamics. The results are less directly transferable to standard public goods games with more players, explicit payoff reporting, or experimental data.

- source: Roy, S., Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Sar, G. K., Banerjee, J., Rakshit, B., Mali, P. C., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2023). Time delays shape the eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can, depending on parameter values (notably the cost of punishment and the temptation to defect), lead to the coexistence of punishers and defectors, dominance of punishers, or extinction of all strategies. Time delay can induce oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, and the ecological 'free space' variable can support the survival of cooperation-related strategies. The fate of cooperation, punishment, and defection is highly sensitive to parameter values and initial conditions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the population dynamics of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment, its cost, and related parameters affect the prevalence of cooperation and punishment in a prisoner's dilemma context with eco-evolutionary feedbacks and time delay. However, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can suppress defection or support cooperation, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Li, B. Q., Wu, Z. X., & Guan, J. Y. (2022). Critical thresholds of benefit distribution in an extended snowdrift game model. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In both infinite and finite structured populations, the critical proportion of benefits that defectors can exploit (while still maintaining stable cooperation) increases with the total resource available, but saturates at a limit. (2) Introducing incentives for cooperators and punishments for defectors increases this critical threshold, meaning that inequality (the share defectors can take) can become worse with such mechanisms. (3) In more highly connected populations, the critical threshold is lower, so inequality is less severe. (4) There is a critical value of incentive/punishment magnitude above which the relationship between resource size and critical threshold reverses. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but rather the conditions for stable coexistence of cooperation and defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment (and reward) on the sustainability of cooperation under unequal benefit distributions in a snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that introducing punishment (and reward) can increase the threshold of tolerated inequality, potentially making outcomes less fair for cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that punishment can have counterintuitive effects on the distribution of benefits and the persistence of cooperation, especially under varying network structures and resource levels.

- source: Ibrahim, A. M. (2022). The conditional defector strategies can violate the most crucial supporting mechanisms of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional defector strategies—cheaters who sometimes cooperate (e.g., to share dispersal costs or pay to escape punishment)—can successfully invade and dominate populations even when mechanisms like punishment, kin selection, and spatial structure are present. In the models, when cheaters can share dispersal costs, they can migrate and outcompete cooperators, leading to cooperator extinction. Similarly, when cheaters reduce their own payoff to avoid detection and punishment, they can dominate despite the presence of punishment mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of agent types and extinction events, not direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee the maintenance of cooperation or high efficiency if conditional defector strategies are possible. The models are adjacent to public goods games and do not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible. However, the findings suggest that in environments where agents can adaptively evade punishment (e.g., by reducing their own payoff to avoid detection), the expected efficiency gain from enabling punishment may be much lower or even negative, as cheaters can still dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper highlights the importance of considering the sophistication of defector strategies and the limitations of punishment as a stabilizing mechanism.

- source: Wylie, J., & Gantman, A. (2024). Cooperation, domination: Twin functions of third-party punishment. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that codified rules and third-party punishment have 'twin functions': they can promote cooperation and social order, but also serve as tools for domination, discrimination, and oppression. The authors review literature showing that third-party punishment can increase cooperation and group payoffs in experimental games, but also highlight evidence that punishment is often motivated by self-interest, ingroup favoritism, and can be used to harm outgroups or marginalized individuals. The paper calls for a broader theoretical understanding of punishment that includes its role in maintaining social hierarchies and facilitating discrimination, not just cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it is useful for contextualizing the dual roles of punishment mechanisms: while they can increase cooperation and efficiency under some conditions (as shown in the literature it reviews), they can also be used in ways that undermine fairness or group welfare, especially when institutionalized. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and may be moderated by group composition, institutional legitimacy, and the potential for discriminatory enforcement. It does not provide parameter-level evidence for any specific game design dimension.

- source: Molho, C., De Petrillo, F., Garfield, Z. H., & Slewe, S. (2024). Cross-societal variation in norm enforcement systems. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews 28 empirical studies on cross-societal variation in punishment and norm enforcement, covering a range of methods (economic games, vignettes, ethnography, surveys). It finds that punishment and norm enforcement are present in many societies, but their prevalence, forms, and social acceptance vary widely. Socio-ecological factors (community size, pathogen prevalence, subsistence type) and cultural factors (individualism/collectivism, honor, kinship intensity, power distance, trust) are discussed as moderators of punishment behaviors. The review notes that evidence on these moderators is often mixed or context-dependent. While some included studies use public goods games with punishment, the review does not aggregate or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and attitudinal measures of punishment and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and contextual background on how punishment and norm enforcement vary across societies and what factors may moderate these behaviors. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, this review may help identify potentially relevant moderators (e.g., community size, cultural values) but does not supply parameter-level evidence or efficiency outcomes needed for direct prediction. It is best used for contextual framing and hypothesis generation rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Fischborn, M. (2023). The Evolutionary Roots of Moral Responsibility. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral responsibility judgment evolved as an adaptation that enabled groups of cooperative individuals to hold free riders responsible more safely by acting in a coordinated way. It distinguishes between two senses of justification in responsibility judgment (demand and permission) and hypothesizes that the permission sense evolved to allow individuals to assess social support for punishment, making collective punishment episodes safer and more stable. The account is situated within Tomasello's two-step theory of the evolution of morality and Boehm's ethnographic work on hunter-gatherer punishment practices. The paper critiques Mameli's account of second-order punishment and meta-emotional dispositions, arguing that coordinated group punishment (rather than second-order punishment) is the key evolutionary innovation. The paper does not provide empirical evidence or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual framework for understanding the robustness and negative bias of responsibility practices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical account of the evolutionary function of punishment and responsibility judgment, emphasizing the importance of coordination and social support in making punishment episodes safer and more stable. While it discusses the role of punishment in stabilizing cooperation and addressing free rider problems, it does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to providing conceptual context about why punishment may have evolved to support cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative or design-specific predictions about efficiency effects in experimental games.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., Fitouchi, L., Baumard, N., & André, J. B. (2024). The social leverage effect: Institutions transform weak reputation effects into strong incentives for cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutions, by leveraging contributions from individuals (motivated by reputation), can create strong enough incentives (via punishment, monitoring, or rewards) to sustain cooperation in settings where reputation alone would fail. The efficiency of the institution (how much incentive is generated per unit contribution) is a key moderator: high efficiency greatly expands the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are possible. The model also shows that when the population is patient (values future reputation), more individuals contribute, further increasing institutional effectiveness. However, when cooperation is easy, the institution can be wasteful, as its costs are not offset by additional gains in cooperation or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling efficient institutional punishment or monitoring in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency and payoffs, especially when baseline (control) efficiency is low due to hard cooperation problems. The model clarifies that the effect of punishment depends critically on the efficiency of the institution (punishment technology), the cost of contributing to the institution, and the patience of the population. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when institutional punishment is efficient and the population is sufficiently future-oriented, enabling punishment will lead to large increases in efficiency relative to control. However, if cooperation is already high in control, the marginal benefit of punishment may be negligible or even negative due to unnecessary costs.

- source: Li, J. M., & Jiang, S. S. (2023). How can governance strategies be developed for marine ecological environment pollution caused by sea-using enterprises?-A study based on evolutionary game theory. *Ocean & Coastal Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of strong governance mechanisms (punishment, compensation, or high ecological performance appraisal), all parties tend to adopt minimal effort strategies, resulting in low efficiency and poor environmental outcomes (prisoner's dilemma). Introducing punishment (penalties for non-compliance, ecological damage compensation), increasing the weight of ecological indicators in local government performance appraisal, and combining these tools can shift the system to a more efficient equilibrium, where local governments and enterprises fully implement and comply with environmental policies, even if central government supervision is weak. The effect of any single tool is limited, but their combination is necessary to achieve high efficiency in governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms (penalties, compensation for damage) and reward mechanisms (performance appraisal) are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from introducing punishment in public-goods-like governance settings. The results suggest that, in adjacent multi-agent settings, punishment alone may not be sufficient, but when combined with other governance tools, it can substantially increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment, reward, and their combination should be considered as key moderators of treatment efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to weak incentives.

- source: Chassy, P., Cole, J., & Brennan, C. (2025). Evolution of confrontation and cooperation in simple organisms as a function of environmental resources and cost of a conflict. *Expert Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The simulations show that the confrontation rate in a population is highly sensitive to the magnitude of punishment (cost of conflict) and reward (resource availability). As punishment increases, confrontation rates decrease, and organisms become more cooperative. The adaptation is driven by the evolution of gene profiles that determine the probability of confrontation. The model also finds that memory (even a simple one-step memory) has a limited but significant effect on confrontation rates, especially as punishment increases. The study provides evidence that risk-taking and confrontation are shaped by both environmental parameters and genetic/cognitive factors, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cost or effectiveness of punishment in a confrontation/cooperation environment reduces the frequency of confrontational (defecting) behavior and increases cooperation. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in environments where punishment is more costly or effective, populations evolve to be more cooperative, which would likely increase efficiency in analogous PGG-like settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff measures limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects in related strategic environments.

- source: Marco, J., & Goetz, R. (2024). Public policy design and common property resources: A social network approach. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social network structure (cohesiveness) and the severity of the resource dilemma jointly determine the effectiveness of informal (social pressure) enforcement in promoting cooperation. Network-oriented policies can reduce the minimum share of compliers needed for full cooperation, but only up to 26% in the calibrated case. Legal enforcement (fines/subsidies) can complement informal enforcement, but subsidizing the costs of social pressure may eliminate stable partial cooperation. The number of agents has only minor effects unless changed drastically. The model suggests that policy effectiveness depends on initial conditions (stock, share of compliers, network structure) and that mixed enforcement strategies should be applied cautiously, as they may interact in non-additive ways.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment (here, social pressure) in public-goods-like common resource games depend on network cohesiveness, the perceived severity of the dilemma, and the cost structure of punishment. The model suggests that punishment can increase the basin of attraction for cooperation and reduce the critical mass of compliers needed, but the effect is bounded. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency (move the system toward the socially optimal steady state), but the magnitude of the effect is limited by network structure and agents' perceptions. The findings are most relevant for settings with costly, decentralized punishment and binary contribution choices, and less so for standard lab PGGs with explicit monetary punishment. The results are qualitative and parameter-dependent, not direct empirical estimates.

- source: Armstrong, K., Zhong, Y. M., Bhide, S., Grant, S. B., Birkland, T., & Berglund, E. Z. (2024). Simulating the emergence of institutions that reverse freshwater salinization: An agent-based modeling approach. *Journal of Hydrology X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The agent-based model demonstrates that self-organized institutions with sanctions (fines for defectors, monitoring) can emerge and stabilize resource use, keeping salt concentrations below harmful thresholds when defection rates are low and innovation rates are moderate to high. High defection rates undermine institution effectiveness, leading to resource degradation. The model highlights the importance of cooperation, monitoring, and sanctions in sustaining common-pool resources, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the emergence and stability of sanctioning institutions in resource dilemmas, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment and monitoring can improve resource outcomes in social dilemmas, especially when defection is not too prevalent and agents can innovate or imitate successful strategies. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate standard PGG design dimensions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like games, this paper offers contextual and mechanistic insights but not direct predictive evidence. Its main value is in illustrating how sanctions and institutional emergence can stabilize cooperation and resource quality in agent-based models of common-pool resource dilemmas.

- source: Li, Q., Pi, B., Feng, M. Y., & Kurths, J. (2024). Open Data in the Digital Economy: An Evolutionary Game Theory Perspective. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of regulatory rewards/penalties (F) can shift the system toward cooperative equilibria, where all parties choose the socially desirable strategies (high-quality data provision, data acquisition, and regulation). The model provides explicit conditions under which full cooperation, partial cooperation, or defection equilibria are stable. Increasing the effectiveness or magnitude of regulatory punishment/reward expands the parameter space in which cooperation is stable. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium strategy fractions, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment/reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in multi-stakeholder environments similar to public goods games, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the strength or effectiveness of regulatory punishment/reward (analogous to punishment_tech and punishment_cost) can promote cooperative behavior among agents in a multi-stakeholder environment. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is informative for predicting that stronger, more effective punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not directly analyzed, the paper should be used as indirect evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, with caution regarding transfer to standard PGG settings.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., Chen, Q., Gao, L. Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Effects of quasi-defection strategy on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
