  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of quasi-defectors—who can both reward cooperators and punish defectors—enables the stable coexistence of cooperation and quasi-defection, and can suppress defection under certain parameter regimes. The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited: increasing punishment initially increases cooperation, but further increases have diminishing returns. Reward can compensate for the limited effect of punishment and further promote cooperation. The cost of rewarding/punishing must be low for quasi-defectors to persist and support cooperation. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that mechanisms combining reward and punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that combining punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, and that the effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited by cost and diminishing returns. However, because the model is based on a prisoner's dilemma with a quasi-defection strategy and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The findings suggest that, in analogous settings, moderate punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and the mapping from strategy proportions to group payoff.

- source: Dong, D. K., & Li, S. (2023). How a Smart regulatory platform can improve the property fee adjustment dilemma. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that under traditional regulation, the game between homeowners and property management tends to a 'prisoner's dilemma' with mutual defection (betrayal). Introducing a smart regulatory platform with government oversight, information transparency, and punishment for non-cooperation shifts the evolutionary stable strategy toward mutual cooperation. Stronger regulation and higher punishment increase the speed and likelihood of cooperation, while higher property fee increases make cooperation less likely. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game, not on empirical data. The outcomes are expressed in terms of cooperation rates, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing punishment and regulatory oversight in a repeated social dilemma can increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is strong and information is transparent. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment and increasing regulatory strength would likely increase cooperation and, by implication, could improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The model's structure and findings may inform predictions about the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, especially regarding the importance of punishment strength and transparency, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the target task.

- source: Pei, H. Y., & Yan, G. H. (2022). Punishment and reputation based partners-switching promotes cooperation in social networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that a mechanism combining behavioral punishment (severing ties with defectors) and reputation-based partner selection leads to high levels of cooperation in a dynamic networked prisoner's dilemma. The frequency of partner switching (time scale ratio W) is a key moderator: higher W leads to higher cooperation, and there is a critical W above which cooperation dominates. The effect is robust to temptation to defect and network degree, though higher values of these parameters require higher W for cooperation to prevail. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that cooperation can be sustained or reach fixation under the right conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, behavioral punishment via partner switching) can promote cooperation in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, the model is a prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where individuals can sever ties with defectors and form new ties based on reputation, cooperation is likely to be higher, especially when partner switching is frequent. For the prediction task, this paper supports the idea that punishment and partner selection mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like settings.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Bi, Y., & Yang, H. (2023). Based on reputation consistent strategy times promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based warning/punishment mechanism—where players are less likely to imitate neighbors who have persistently defected—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG simulation. Stronger punishment (higher discount factor) and more timely punishment (lower threshold for triggering the discount) both further increase cooperation rates. The effect is robust across a range of temptation-to-defect parameters. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms targeting persistent defectors can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding mechanisms that increase cooperation, but should be used with caution for efficiency prediction, as higher cooperation does not always translate to higher group payoff depending on the cost structure of punishment and the payoff matrix.

- source: Goodman, J. R., Caines, A., & Foley, R. A. (2023). Shibboleth: An agent-based model of signalling mimicry. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The models show that increasing the sensitivity to mimicry (the ability to detect outsiders) is a much more effective way to maintain group-defining traits than increasing the cost imposed on detected mimics. Punishment mechanisms (altruistic punishment of in-group members who are insufficiently sensitive) can help maintain group traits, but their effect is less dramatic than that of detection sensitivity. The cost of punishment, even when high, does not strongly predict which trait will go to fixation. The models do not report group efficiency or payoff, but focus on trait fixation as a proxy for group integrity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. However, it provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation (or group integrity) depends more on the likelihood of detection than on the severity of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment mechanisms that increase detection or monitoring may be more effective than those that simply increase punishment cost. The findings are most relevant to environments where group identity or norm compliance is the main outcome, rather than efficiency or payoff.

- source: Wang, S. Y., Yao, X., Yang, Y. M., Chen, D. N., Wang, R. W., & Xie, F. J. (2023). Super-rational aspiration promotes cooperation in the asymmetric game with peer exit punishment and reward. *Heliyon*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in structured populations, higher super-rational aspiration and greater asymmetry both promote the prevalence of cooperation (i.e., higher proportion of cooperators) and participation (i.e., fewer loners). Exit punishment (negative exit payoff) and exit reward (positive exit payoff) have distinct effects: exit punishment, combined with high super-rationality, can lead to coexistence of cooperators, defectors, and loners, while exit reward tends to result in coexistence of defectors and loners at low super-rationality, but can also promote cooperation at higher super-rationality. The effect of selection intensity is quantitative, not qualitative. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that peer-based exit punishment and reward, as well as super-rational aspiration and asymmetry, can influence the prevalence of cooperation and participation in public-goods-like social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or welfare. Thus, the paper is useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms but provides only indirect, non-quantitative support for efficiency prediction.

- source: Xue, B. Y., & Hu, Y. H. (2025). Dynamic analysis of evolutionary game considering individual influence. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing influential cooperators, who can punish defectors (via a payoff penalty theta), can suppress defection and promote cooperation in a population. The prevalence of cooperation, influential cooperation, and defection depends on the temptation to defect (b), the strength of punishment (theta), and the weight individuals place on payoff versus influence (rho). Higher punishment for defectors (theta) reduces the proportion of defectors and can eliminate them entirely at high values, leading to more cooperation. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on overall welfare is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that punishment (modeled as a penalty for defectors) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment parameters (such as cost and effectiveness) on cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, J. W., Xu, W. S., Zhang, X. J., Zhao, N. X., & Yu, F. Y. (2023). Redistribution based on willingness to cooperate promotes cooperation while intensifying equality in heterogeneous populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can persist in the presence of corrupt central sanctioning institutions, provided the effectiveness of sanctions (exclusion probability) is high and the corruption rate (embezzlement) is not too large. The model shows that players are more willing to tolerate exploitation by corrupt third parties than by free-riders, and that the evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the balance between the benefits of cooperation, the costs of joining/leaving the institution, the intensity of exclusion, and the corruption rate. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and trust strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (exclusion) and the cost/benefit structure are critical for sustaining cooperation, even when institutions are corrupt. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The results imply that punishment can support cooperation under a range of conditions, but the translation to efficiency is not explicit. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure (prisoner's dilemma with exclusion and embezzlement).

- source: Schlapfer, A. (2024). Cross-cultural differences in retaliation: Evidence from the soccer field. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds, at a theoretical level, that polycentric governance structures are more likely to support the emergence and stability of group-beneficial norms, including costly punishment, by satisfying four key conditions: (1) minimizing within-group variance, (2) reducing within-group selective pressures against prosocial traits (e.g., via effective punishment), (3) increasing between-group selective pressures, and (4) maximizing between-group variance. It argues that punishment can transform altruistic traits into individually rational behaviors when punishment is widespread and coordinated, and that polycentricity supports the self-sorting and coordination needed for this. However, the paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment, when embedded in polycentric or self-sorting group structures, can enhance cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency or welfare depends on the ability of groups to self-sort, coordinate, and monitor, and on the cost structure of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, self-sorting, and the scaling of punishment costs, rather than in providing parameterized estimates.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & Wu, J. H. (2022). Direct and indirect reciprocity among individuals and groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that group membership, gossip, and third-party punishment promote prosocial behavior through psychological mechanisms related to direct and indirect reciprocity. Third-party punishment is framed as a tool for upregulating reputation and deterring mistreatment, which in turn increases prosocial behavior among others. The review highlights that reputational concern and anticipation of future interaction are key drivers of both prosocial and punitive behaviors. However, the review does not report or synthesize results on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and focuses on behavioral and psychological outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment (especially third-party punishment) can increase prosocial behavior, but it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., reputation, observability, group membership), but does not inform direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as background on psychological mechanisms and as a pointer to empirical work, rather than as a source of quantitative or design-specific evidence.

- source: Odouard, V. V., & Price, M. H. (2023). Tit for tattling: Cooperation, communication, and how each could stabilize the other. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with indirect reciprocity, if agents use an aligned norm (rewarding the behaviors it prescribes), can signal about both actions and the truthfulness of signals (meta-signaling), and make occasional errors, then a stable equilibrium emerges with near-maximal cooperation and efficiency. The model shows that any deviation from the prescribed strategy is costly, and the proportion of non-cooperation (and thus inefficiency) can be made arbitrarily small by reducing error rates. The equilibrium is robust to invasion by alternative strategies, and the system achieves high group payoff and efficiency. The findings are theoretical and based on mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments with indirect reciprocity, the introduction of mechanisms that allow for social enforcement (meta-signaling) and some error can stabilize highly efficient, cooperative equilibria. While the model is not a public goods game per se, the mapping to efficiency and group payoff is direct and informative for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, social enforcement via meta-signaling) on efficiency. The results suggest that, under the right institutional and informational conditions, enabling social enforcement can substantially increase efficiency relative to baseline. However, the model is highly stylized and assumes infinite population, infinite rounds, and well-mixed interactions, so predictions should be adjusted for more realistic settings.

- source: Murase, Y., & Hilbe, C. (2024). Computational evolution of social norms in well-mixed and group-structured populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in well-mixed populations, even with complex (third-order) social norms, cooperation and high group payoff (efficiency) are difficult to sustain: defecting strategies (ALLD) are most abundant, and average payoffs are low. In group-structured populations, however, cooperation and high efficiency can evolve and persist, especially when third-order norms are available. The most successful norm (L1) treats cooperation as always good and defection as bad, except when defection is 'justified' (against bad recipients). This norm is robust to disagreements and errors in private assessment. The results highlight the importance of population structure and norm complexity for the evolution of efficient, cooperative outcomes. The model does not implement explicit costly punishment, but some norms functionally resemble punishment by allowing justified defection without reputational cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the population (well-mixed vs. group-structured) and the complexity of available social norms (second-order vs. third-order) are critical moderators of the effect of reputational 'punishment' (justified defection) on group efficiency. In well-mixed populations, enabling more complex norms or reputational punishment does not reliably increase efficiency; in group-structured populations, it can lead to high efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of group structure and the richness of the norm/strategy space are key predictors of whether enabling reputational punishment will improve efficiency. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, as the model is dyadic and punishment is reputational, not material.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2022). Evolution of trustfulness in the case where resources for cooperation are sometimes absent. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with stochastic absence of resources for cooperation, the evolutionarily stable outcome is often a coexistence of trustful cooperators and trustful defectors, provided the cost-to-benefit ratio, the probability of lacking resources, and the exogenous break-up rate are not too high. Trustful strategies (those that keep interacting with partners who defect due to lack of resources) are robustly favored by natural selection, and this leads to higher equilibrium frequencies of cooperation and higher group payoffs (efficiency). When parameters are unfavorable (high cost, high resource absence, high break-up), defectors dominate and efficiency is low. The model does not include explicit punishment or costly sanctioning, but the 'walk-away' (partner switching) mechanism is functionally adjacent to punishment in that it can enforce cooperation by breaking ties with defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not model a public goods game or explicit punishment, it analyzes a closely related repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with partner choice (walk-away) and stochastic resource constraints. The key design dimensions mapped are the cost-to-benefit ratio (analogous to MPCR), the probability of resource absence (a form of noise or cooperation constraint), and the exogenous break-up rate (analogous to the probability of game continuation or group stability). The main result is that mechanisms allowing players to break ties with defectors (even when defection is due to lack of resources) can support high efficiency, provided the game is not too harsh (low cost, low noise, stable groups). This suggests that in PGG-like environments, enabling partner choice or walk-away (an adjacent form of sanction) can increase efficiency, especially when the baseline efficiency is not too low. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the logic of positive assortment and the conditions for efficiency gains are highly relevant.

- source: Pei, H. (2024). Reputation Effects under Short Memories. *Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with a patient player and a sequence of short-run players who observe only summary statistics of the last K actions (not the full sequence), the patient player can always secure at least his commitment payoff by building a reputation, regardless of K. However, the welfare of the short-run players (i.e., group efficiency) is maximized in all equilibria only if K is below a threshold (cutoff). For K above the cutoff, there exist equilibria where the short-run players' welfare is strictly below first-best, because longer memory makes it harder to motivate short-run players to punish deviations. Thus, increasing the memory window K can have a non-monotonic or even negative effect on group efficiency. The results are robust to various extensions, including imperfect monitoring and coarser information structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in repeated games with reputation and limited memory, the ability of short-run players to punish deviations (and thus sustain high efficiency) depends critically on the memory length K. If K is too large, the threat of punishment becomes less effective, and group efficiency can fall below first-best in some equilibria. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where punishment is implemented via reputation and memory is limited to summary statistics, increasing the memory window does not necessarily improve efficiency and may even reduce it. The results are most directly relevant to settings where efficiency is determined by the ability to sustain cooperation through reputation and punishment, rather than direct PGG lab experiments. The findings are less directly applicable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment stages, but the mechanism insights are informative for predicting efficiency in repeated or reputation-based games.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Lütz, A. F., Amaral, M. A., Braga, I., & Wardil, L. (2023). Invasion of Optimal Social Contracts. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population playing a stag-hunt game (representing a social contract), the introduction of a moderate diversity incentive (modeled as a snowdrift game in a focal group) allows the population to overcome the invasion barrier and reach the globally optimal equilibrium (all adopting the efficient norm A). This transition increases group efficiency (total payoff). If the diversity incentive is too strong, the system may stabilize at a mixed equilibrium, but turning off the incentive after the transition can still result in full adoption of the optimal norm. The effect is strongest when the number of group connections is moderate; if individuals are too highly connected, the social pressure of the stag-hunt dominates and the incentive is less effective. The findings are supported by analytical and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that introducing a moderate incentive for diversity (analogous to a reward or tolerance for deviance) can increase group efficiency in coordination games with multiple equilibria, by enabling the population to escape suboptimal equilibria. While the model does not directly study public goods games or punishment, it is adjacent in that it addresses how incentive structures and group connectivity affect the ability to reach efficient outcomes. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that mechanisms which lower the cost or risk of deviating from the majority (even temporarily) can facilitate transitions to higher-efficiency equilibria, especially in moderately connected groups. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so direct quantitative predictions for punishment-enabled PGGs are not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Salahshour, M., & Couzin, I. D. (2025). Evolution of altruistic rationality provides a solution to social dilemmas via rational reciprocity. *Physical Review Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where agents can evolve their degree of other-regarding preferences (altruism), populations transition from mutual defection (low efficiency) to high cooperation (high efficiency) in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma, provided the benefit of cooperation is sufficiently high. This transition is robust to population structure and does not require explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. Instead, 'punishment' of selfishness occurs via the rational withholding of cooperation by conditional cooperators. The resulting group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) increases substantially compared to the Nash equilibrium of selfish agents. The findings generalize across different two-person two-strategy games, with the mechanism being most pronounced in the Prisoner's Dilemma and also effective in the Snowdrift and Stag Hunt games.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that allowing for the evolution of other-regarding preferences (subjective rationality) can lead to high efficiency in social dilemmas, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. In environments structurally similar to two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, Snowdrift, or Stag Hunt games, if agents can recognize and respond to others' types, group efficiency can approach the cooperative optimum. However, the model does not include explicit punishment costs, technologies, or public goods game features such as multi-player interactions, continuous contributions, or explicit sanctioning. Thus, while the findings are highly informative about the potential for endogenous cooperation and efficiency gains, they are only indirectly applicable to standard public goods games with explicit punishment treatments. The main implication is that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional cooperation can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting efficiency.

- source: Peña, J., Heifetz, A., & Nöldeke, G. (2024). The shirker's dilemma and the prospect of cooperation in large groups. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the shirker's dilemma, as group size increases, the proportion of volunteers at the stable equilibrium increases (for low enough costs), but the expected payoff (efficiency) decreases. The probability that the public good is provided can either increase or decrease with group size, depending on the cost of cooperation. In the limit of very large groups and low costs, the proportion of volunteers approaches one, and the expected payoff and success probability converge to positive values. However, the basin of attraction for the cooperative equilibrium shrinks with group size, making cooperation less likely to be reached from random initial conditions. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a binary-action threshold collective action game (shirker's dilemma), increasing group size generally reduces efficiency (expected payoff at equilibrium), even though the proportion of volunteers may increase. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, larger groups are less efficient, and the probability of successful collective action may not improve with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar threshold public goods environments without punishment, efficiency will decrease as group size increases. However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does provide a theoretical baseline for control (no-punishment) conditions in threshold games.

- source: Kroumi, D. (2025). The role of selective extinction in promoting cooperation in a many demes model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that structuring a population into demes and introducing selective extinction—whereby groups with more defectors are more likely to go extinct—can dramatically increase the evolutionary success (fixation probability) of cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. Without selective extinction (\alpha=0), defection is always favored, and cooperation cannot persist. As the selective extinction parameter \alpha increases, the fixation probability of cooperation increases, and that of defection decreases. There are explicit threshold values of \alpha (functions of game payoffs and demographic parameters) above which cooperation is favored by selection, and above which it is more likely to fix than defection. The model also shows how the baseline extinction rate (v) and the structure of payoffs (T-R, P-S) modulate these thresholds. The results are robust to several alternative life-cycle assumptions. The main outcome is that group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction) can overcome the individual-level advantage of defection and promote group-level efficiency (cooperation).
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in structured populations with group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction of defect-dominated groups), the efficiency of the system (measured by the fixation probability of cooperation and thus long-run group payoff) can be substantially increased by increasing the strength of selective extinction (\alpha). In the absence of such feedback, efficiency remains low (defection dominates). The model gives explicit formulas for the critical value of \alpha needed to shift the system from defection-dominated to cooperation-dominated, as a function of game payoffs and demographic parameters (deme size, number of demes, baseline extinction rate). While the model is not a direct public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, the selective extinction process is functionally analogous to a group-level sanction. Thus, the findings support the prediction that introducing or strengthening group-level sanctions (even if not individual punishment) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the control (no-sanction) efficiency is low.

- source: Gokhale, C. S., Frean, M., & Rainey, P. B. (2023). Eco-evolutionary Logic of Mutualisms. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability and persistence of mutualisms (modeled as multiplayer snowdrift games) are strongly influenced by the balance between intra- and inter-species interactions, as well as by ecological factors such as seasonality and population density. The inclusion of within-species interactions and eco-evolutionary feedbacks can stabilize mutualisms and maintain high group-level productivity (efficiency) even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The model shows that nonlinearities, group size, and environmental fluctuations can qualitatively change the evolutionary outcomes, sometimes allowing coexistence of cooperative and selfish types, and sometimes leading to dominance or extinction. The framework is general and extensible, but the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency of multiplayer public-goods-like games (here, mutualisms modeled as snowdrift games) can be maintained or stabilized by ecological and evolutionary feedbacks, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. It suggests that predictions of efficiency should account for intra- and inter-group interactions, group size, seasonality, and density-dependence. However, because the paper does not model or analyze punishment or sanctioning directly, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its main value for the prediction task is in highlighting the importance of ecological and evolutionary context, and in providing a general modeling framework that could be extended to include punishment or reward.

- source: Morsky, B., Plotkin, J. B., & Akcay, E. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with Bayesian reasoning and biases. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Bayesian reasoning about errors in action and observation generally reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff in indirect reciprocity games, compared to models where agents do not reason about errors. This effect is robust across most social norms (Shunning, Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), but under the Scoring norm, Bayesian reasoning can actually promote cooperation and support a mixed equilibrium with all strategy types. The impact of Bayesian reasoning is strongest when error rates are low. Optimism bias (overestimating the frequency of good individuals) generally undermines cooperation, while mild pessimism bias can sometimes promote it, but strong biases of either type destroy cooperation. The model provides heatmaps and bifurcation diagrams showing how cooperation and payoffs change with error rates and biases. The findings are relevant for predicting how changes in information processing, error rates, and social norms affect group efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment/reward mechanisms based on reputation. It shows that the cognitive sophistication of agents (Bayesian reasoning about errors) can reduce group efficiency, except under certain social norms (Scoring). The results suggest that, in environments where punishment/reward is reputation-based and subject to errors, enabling more sophisticated reasoning may not always increase efficiency. The effect of punishment (via conditional defection) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the social norm, error rates, and the presence of optimism/pessimism bias. For downstream prediction, the paper supports the view that punishment mechanisms relying on reputation can backfire or be less effective if agents reason about errors, unless the norm is simple (Scoring). The findings are most transferable to settings where punishment is implemented as conditional cooperation/defection based on reputation, rather than direct costly punishment.

- source: Graser, C., Fujiwara-Greve, T., García, J., & van Veelen, M. (2025). Repeated games with partner choice. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the option to leave one's partner in repeated prisoner's dilemma games (i.e., partner choice) leads to a substantial increase in average cooperation rates across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and continuation probabilities. This is because partner choice enables endogenous assortment: cooperators can avoid defectors by leaving, which stabilizes cooperation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations both show that, with partner choice, the population spends more time in cooperative states and escapes from universal defection more quickly. The paper compares strategies that punish by defecting (grim trigger) to those that punish by leaving, and finds that punishing by leaving is more robust to invasion and more likely to support cooperation. Although the main outcome is cooperation rate, higher cooperation implies higher average payoffs, so efficiency is indirectly improved by partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that enabling partner choice (the ability to leave and rematch) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the mechanism is closely related, and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where partner choice is possible. The paper does not study costly punishment or explicit sanctions, but rather 'punishment' by leaving. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling partner choice should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no partner choice, especially in repeated dyadic games. The results are most directly relevant when the game is dyadic, repeated, and partner choice is the main intervention.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of aggressive behavior on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing aggressive behavior (a form of costly punishment) into a prisoner's dilemma game can promote the persistence of cooperation by deterring defectors. Aggressive cooperators are able to suppress defectors, especially when the benefit of aggression (compensation) is high and the risk (mutual loss) is not too great. The system can reach various equilibria, including coexistence of multiple strategies, depending on parameter values. When the temptation to defect is low and aggressive compensation is high, cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, defectors re-emerge, but aggressive defectors can also help limit the dominance of non-aggressive defectors, allowing some cooperation to survive. The findings suggest that punishment-like mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to a baseline with only cooperation and defection, though the paper does not quantify efficiency in the standard sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that introducing a punishment-like mechanism (aggressive behavior) in a prisoner's dilemma environment can increase group payoff and support cooperation, especially when the cost-benefit tradeoff of punishment is favorable and the temptation to defect is not too high. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient effectiveness) is likely to improve efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, particularly in environments where the risk of mutual punishment is not prohibitive. However, the lack of explicit efficiency ratios and the focus on evolutionary stability rather than direct payoff comparisons limit the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Shuvo, M. F. R., & Kabir, K. M. A. (2024). Investigating the impact of environmental feedback on the optional prisoner's dilemma for insights into cyclic dominance and evolution of cooperation. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a loner (abstention) strategy and environmental feedback into a two-player, three-strategy game fundamentally alters the evolutionary dynamics compared to the classic prisoner's dilemma. Defection is no longer evolutionarily stable; instead, cyclic dominance and coexistence of strategies emerge, especially under certain environmental conditions. The state of the environment (replenished or depleted) and feedback parameters (replenishment rate, draw payoff) determine whether the system exhibits pure strategy dominance, coexistence, oscillations, or chaos. The model shows that the option to abstain and environmental constraints can promote cooperation and prevent the dominance of defection, but does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or total welfare as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how adding abstention and environmental feedback to social dilemma games can suppress defection and promote more complex, often cooperative, population dynamics. However, because the model is not a public goods game (it is a two-player, three-strategy game) and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment is limited. The findings suggest that introducing additional strategies (like abstention) or environmental feedback can qualitatively change the evolutionary stability of cooperation and defection, but do not provide quantitative evidence or parameter estimates for efficiency outcomes in the context of public goods games with punishment. Thus, this paper is more useful for conceptual framing and mechanism discussion than for direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2025). Reciprocity evolves more readily in competitive than cooperative socio-ecologies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: Reciprocal matching strategies, which reward cooperation and punish defection, are more likely to evolve and persist in competitive socio-ecologies where the risk of exploitation is high (e.g., Chicken/Hawk-Dove/Attack-Defend games) than in cooperative ones (Stag Hunt). In cooperative environments, unconditional cooperation dominates, while in competitive environments, anti-reciprocity (mismatching) and defection are more successful. The success of reciprocity increases with the probability of repeated interaction. The paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, but the evolutionary success of reciprocity implies it is adaptive in competitive settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (as part of reciprocal strategies) in promoting cooperation depends on the competitiveness of the environment and the risk of exploitation. While it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, it suggests that punishment (as part of reciprocity) is more likely to be adaptive and prevalent in competitive, high-risk environments. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be more positive in competitive, high-exploitation-risk games (e.g., Chicken, Hawk-Dove) than in cooperative games (e.g., Stag Hunt). However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Li, S. J., Bao, W., Dai, Y. Y., Ye, Y., & Xie, N. G. (2022). Emotional game and the evolution of cooperation considering the effects of reputation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation mechanism based on emotional profiles to a voluntary prisoner's dilemma game (VPDG) substantially increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the population. The reputation mechanism works by making individuals more likely to cooperate with those who have good reputations and less likely to cooperate with those who have bad reputations, effectively integrating both reward and punishment effects. Compared to previous models (VPDG alone and VPDG with emotion but no reputation), the model with reputation achieves higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation, especially in regions of the parameter space where cooperation would otherwise be low. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is not too high (T < 1.5), but even at higher temptations, the reputation mechanism maintains higher cooperation and payoff than the baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that mechanisms integrating both reward and punishment via reputation can substantially increase group efficiency in voluntary public-goods-like games. While the model is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure (VPDG with loner option) and the explicit reporting of average payoff as a welfare/efficiency measure make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of adding punishment/reward/reputation mechanisms in similar environments. The results suggest that, when the control (no reputation) game has moderate or low efficiency, enabling a reputation mechanism (which includes both positive and negative sanctions) can drive efficiency close to the cooperative maximum, especially when the temptation to defect is not extreme. However, the findings are based on simulation, not human experiments, and the specific implementation of reputation is more complex than simple punishment. The model does not directly manipulate all design dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, etc.), so predictions should be adjusted accordingly.

- source: Milinski, M. (2022). Extortion - A voracious prosocial strategy. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that extortionate strategies in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games can enforce high cooperation from coplayers, but at the cost of an unequal payoff distribution favoring the extortioner. When coplayers attempt to punish extortioners by withholding cooperation, both parties' payoffs decrease, demonstrating a form of costly punishment. In symmetric settings, this can discipline extortioners, but in asymmetric settings (e.g., with replacement power or extra incentives), extortioners can persist and maintain higher payoffs. The review highlights that the efficiency of the group (total payoff) may not increase with extortion, as the gains are captured by the extortioner and punishment reduces total surplus. The findings are based on a synthesis of theory and several experimental studies, but the review itself does not report new experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, costly punishment via withholding cooperation) on efficiency depends on the symmetry of the game and the power structure. In symmetric games, punishment can reduce the extortioner's advantage but at a cost to both players, lowering efficiency. In asymmetric games, extortioners can avoid being disciplined and extract higher payoffs, further reducing the coplayer's share. The review does not directly address public goods games or classic punishment institutions, but the mechanisms discussed (costly punishment, power asymmetry, incentive structures) are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve or reduce efficiency in related environments. The evidence is more about distributional outcomes and the limits of punishment than about average efficiency increases from punishment.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2023). The dynamics of corruption under an optional external supervision service. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an optional external supervision service can reduce corruption and promote cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of players and enforcers. Lowering the cost of supervision is not always optimal: if the cost is too low, unnecessary supervision may occur, while if it is too high, corruption may persist. The initial fraction of honest enforcers and the relative mutation rates are critical: higher mutation rates for enforcers (relative to players) favor honest enforcement and trusting cooperation. In finite populations, the system may reach stable equilibria or exhibit persistent oscillations in the fractions of cooperators, defectors, and corrupt enforcers. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment, corruption, and external supervision interact in a social dilemma context with bribery and enforcement. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff as a function of game design dimensions. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (via external supervision) is highly sensitive to the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of enforcers and players. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not efficiency. Thus, the paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment and corruption than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Zhang, X. C., & Lu, S. C. (2024). The effect of survival pressure-based defector reward behavior on cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that when defectors are forced by survival pressure to reward cooperators, the frequency of cooperation in the population can increase, especially when the survival pressure threshold is low (players are more sensitive to pressure), the reward cost is low, and the reward amount is sufficiently high. The effect of these parameters is non-linear and depends on their combination with the temptation to defect. The model shows that rewarding behavior by defectors acts as a transitional strategy that can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on agent-based simulations on a lattice network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are limited to the effect of a specific form of reward (not punishment) on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The model and results are adjacent to the target prediction task, as they do not include payoff-based outcomes or public goods game structures, nor do they manipulate or analyze punishment. The main relevance is in showing that certain forms of reward can increase cooperation rates under specific parameter regimes, but this cannot be directly translated into efficiency predictions for PGGs with punishment.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Wang, Q., & Mao, C. M. (2024). Research on Cooperative Water Pollution Governance Based on Tripartite Evolutionary Game in China's Yangtze River Basin. *Water*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the evolution toward cooperative governance (strict regulation, green production, active public participation) is promoted by lower regulatory costs, higher fines for non-compliance, appropriate subsidies for green production, and material rewards for public participation. Excessively high or low subsidies or rewards can destabilize cooperation. The model identifies five equilibrium points, with the ideal being all stakeholders choosing the cooperative strategy. The findings are based on theoretical modeling and simulation, not empirical data or observed payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment (fines), rewards, and regulatory costs affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-stakeholder environmental governance setting. While the model structure is related to public goods games and includes punishment and reward analogues, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it provide empirical estimates. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the effects of punishment and reward mechanisms, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes in PGG-like experiments.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Testori, M., Giardini, F., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2023). What Matters Most in Supporting Cooperation, the Gossip Content or the Gossiper's Intention? Simulating Motive Interpretation in Gossip Dynamics. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on how agents interpret the motives behind gossip and how they update reputations. If gossip is interpreted as pro-social and agents use a single reputation system, cooperation can rise to high levels. If gossip is interpreted as selfish or negative, a double reputation system (where agents discount unreliable gossip but do not punish the gossiper by refusing to cooperate) helps maintain initial cooperation levels. The model highlights the importance of motive interpretation and the structure of reputation systems for sustaining cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it offers indirect insights into how information transmission, reputation updating, and the interpretation of motives can moderate the effects of social information on cooperation. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which allow agents to discount unreliable information without ostracizing the source may help sustain cooperation, but the model's outcomes are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of confidence in aspiration-fulfillment strategy on prisoner's dilemma evolution. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a confidence mechanism—where confidence in a strategy is updated based on the difference between payoff and aspiration—can promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
